<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove the Euclidean-complete affine Bernstein conjecture in dimensions three through nine: a smooth locally uniformly convex affine maximal graph is an elliptic paraboloid whenever its induced Euclidean metric is complete. The same conclusion holds, without an initial graph assumption, for connected smooth open (noncompact and without boundary) affine-complete locally uniformly convex immersed hypersurfaces that are classically affine maximal in these dimensions.

- **claim-main-rigidity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Let $3\le n\le9$. Suppose $u\in C^\infty(\Omega)$ has positive-definite Hessian and satisfies <a href="#eq:affine-maximal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-maximal">[eq:affine-maximal]</a>. If its graph is complete for the induced Euclidean metric $$g_{ij}=\delta_{ij}+u_i u_j,$$ then $\Omega=\mathbb R^n$ and $$u(x)=\tfrac12 x^{\mathsf T}Ax+b\cdot x+c$$ for a positive-definite symmetric matrix $A$, a vector $b$, and a constant $c$. In particular, the graph is an invertible affine image of the standard elliptic paraboloid.
  - Explanation: A smooth, locally uniformly convex affine maximal graph whose induced Euclidean metric is complete must be defined on the whole space and be a quadratic polynomial, so its graph is an affine image of the elliptic paraboloid. The range of dimensions is three through nine.
- **claim-proper-sections** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-proper-sections)): At every finite boundary point $b\in\partial\Omega$, $$\lim_{\Omega\ni x\to b}u(x)=+\infty.$$ The epigraph $$D=\{(x,z):x\in\Omega,\ z\ge u(x)\}$$ is a closed full-dimensional convex set with boundary equal to the graph of $u$. For every $a\in\Omega$ and $t\ge0$, the set $\{x\in\Omega:g_a(x)\le t\}$ is compact and contained in $\Omega$. Consequently the cap $D\cap\{z-l_a(x)\le t\}$ is compact.
  - Explanation: Completeness of the induced metric forces the function to blow up at finite boundary points, so the epigraph is closed and tangent sublevel sets and caps are compact inside the domain.
- **claim-cap-compactness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-cap-compactness)): Suppose $C_j\to C$ locally, and let $\ell$ be an affine function. If $C\cap\{\ell\le b\}$ is bounded and contains a point $q$ with $\ell(q)<b$, then the caps $C_j\cap\{\ell\le b\}$ are eventually uniformly bounded. They converge in Hausdorff distance to $C\cap\{\ell\le b\}$.
  - Explanation: If closed convex sets converge locally and the limiting cap is bounded with a point strictly below the cut level, the approximating caps are eventually uniformly bounded and converge in Hausdorff distance.
- **claim-model-fibers** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-model-fibers)): For a $k$-model, addition of any vector $(v,0)$ with $v_i\ge0$ preserves $C$. Every fiber $K_s$ with $s_i>0$ is compact, full-dimensional in $\mathbb R^m$, and contains zero. Moreover, $$\label{eq:fiber-monotonicity} K_s\subset K_t\quad(s_i\le t_i), \qquad K_{\lambda s}\subset\lambda K_s\quad(\lambda\ge1).$$ At least one $1$-model exists.
  - Explanation: In a model, adding nonnegative base directions preserves the body, positive fibers are compact, full-dimensional and contain zero, they are monotone in the base variable, and a one-direction model always exists.
- **claim-linear-normalization** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-linear-normalization)): Let $K\subset\mathbb R^d$, $d\ge1$, be compact and convex with nonempty interior and $0\in K$. There are an invertible linear map $A$ and a vector $c$ such that $$\label{eq:linear-normalization} \overline B_1(c)\subset AK\subset\overline B_d(c), \qquad \|c\|\le d.$$ In particular $AK\subset\overline B_{2d}$; the origin is allowed to lie on $\partial K$. If in addition $-K\subset C_*K$ for some $C_*\ge1$, then $$\label{eq:centered-normalization} \overline B_{2/(C_*+1)} \subset AK\subset\overline B_{2d}.$$
  - Explanation: A linear version of John's ellipsoid theorem normalizes a compact convex body containing the origin between two balls, with a centered inner ball when a centering inclusion holds.
- **claim-uniform-centering** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-centering)): Suppose $m=n+1-k>0$. There is a constant $C_*\ge1$, depending on the family $\mathcal F(D)$, such that $$\label{eq:model-centering} -K_s\subset C_*K_s \qquad(s_i>0)$$ for every $k$-model and every admissible choice of its coordinates. In particular, zero is an interior point of every such fiber.
  - Explanation: When the number of directions is maximal, positive fibers satisfy a uniform centering inclusion; failure would produce a model with one more direction.
- **claim-normalized-fibers** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-normalized-fibers)): Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:centering" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:centering">7</a>, any chosen positive fiber of any $k$-model can be moved to $s=\mathbf1$ by positive diagonal changes in $s$ and normalized by an invertible linear change in $y$ so that $$\overline B_{r_0}\subset K_{\mathbf1} \subset\overline B_{R_0}, \qquad r_0=\frac2{C_*+1},\quad R_0=2m.$$ For every fixed $0<a<1<b$, these normalized models satisfy $$\label{eq:normalized-fiber-box} \overline B_{ar_0}\subset K_s \subset\overline B_{bR_0} \qquad(s\in[a,b]^k).$$ Their caps for fixed positive linear forms in $s$ and fixed positive top levels have uniform bounds, and they contain a fixed interior ball about $(\mathbf1,0)$.* *For any sequence of affine images of $D$ converging to one of these models, comparable cap and fiber bounds hold eventually, uniformly on each fixed positive box. The constants are independent of the chosen fiber; the required index may depend on its normalization. For finitely many chosen fibers, one index suffices for all of them.
  - Explanation: After diagonal and linear normalization, fibers over fixed positive boxes lie between two balls with constants independent of the chosen fiber, and the same bounds hold eventually for approximating affine images.
- **claim-stationarity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-stationarity)): The equation in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> implies stationarity of affine area under every smooth compactly supported variation of the graph. Every invertible affine image of the graph is stationary under such variations as well.
  - Explanation: The affine maximal equation means stationarity of affine area under compactly supported variations, and this persists for every invertible affine image of the graph.
- **claim-area-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-area-bound)): For each $n$ and $R>0$, the affine area of the part in $B_R$ of any smooth positively curved convex boundary in $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ is bounded by a constant depending only on $n,R$.
  - Explanation: The affine area of a smooth positively curved convex boundary inside a ball is bounded by a constant depending only on the dimension and the radius.
- **claim-cap-identity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-cap-identity)): Let $M$ be an affine image of the original graph, bounding its convex body $D'$. Let $o\in\operatorname{int}D'$, let $\ell$ be an affine function on the ambient space, and let $\psi\in C^\infty(\mathbb R)$. Suppose that the restrictions of $\psi(\ell),\psi'(\ell),\psi''(\ell)$ vanish outside a compact subset of $M$. Then $$\label{eq:cap-identity} \int_M\left[ \nu(o-X)\|\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\ell\|_{H^{-1}}^2\psi''(\ell) +n\bigl(\ell(o)-\ell(X)\bigr)\psi'(\ell) -n(n+1)\psi(\ell) \right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm dA=0.$$ Here $\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\ell$ is restricted to the tangent space. The first term does not depend on the scaling of the inward conormal.
  - Explanation: Stationarity tested against a suitable function yields an exact integral identity on affine images of the graph linking the inverse second form to cutoff functions of an affine function.
- **claim-log-inequality** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-log-inequality)): Let $3\le n\le9$ and $2\le k\le n$. On any tube satisfying the hypotheses of this section, there is a constant $C_n$, depending only on $n$, such that every $\widetilde v\in C_c^\infty(\log\mathcal B)$ satisfies $$\label{eq:log-inequality} \int_{\mathcal T}\widetilde v(\log s)^2\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\sigma \le C_n\int_{\mathcal T} \|\nabla\widetilde v(\log s)\|_{\mathbb R^k}^2\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\sigma.$$ Here $\log s=(\log s_1,\ldots,\log s_k)$ and the gradient on the right is Euclidean. No completeness of the metric $G$ is required.
  - Explanation: On a convex tube in dimensions three through nine, a Poincaré-type inequality holds in logarithmic coordinates with a constant depending only on the dimension.
- **claim-one-direction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-one-direction)): Suppose $3\le n\le9$. The largest number of directions of a model in $\mathcal F(D)$, in the sense of Definition <a href="#def:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:model">4</a>, is one.
  - Explanation: Models with two or more directions are excluded because polynomial growth of a logarithmic mass contradicts exponential growth forced by the logarithmic inequality.
- **claim-positive-cap-area** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-positive-cap-area)): Let $D_j$ be affine images of $D$ converging locally to a full-dimensional closed convex set $C$. Suppose that a nonzero linear function $\ell$ and a number $b$ satisfy $$C\cap\{\ell\le b\}\ \text{is compact}, \qquad \operatorname{int}C\cap\{\ell<b\}\ne\varnothing.$$ There are $c>0$ and $j_0$ such that $$\int_{\partial D_j\cap\{\ell<b\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm dA_j\ge c \qquad(j\ge j_0).$$
  - Explanation: Affine area of approximating caps stays bounded away from zero, by comparing with a quadratic perturbation using concavity of the area integrand and stationarity.
- **claim-log-cell-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-log-cell-bound)): There is a constant $C_{\mathrm{cell}}$, independent of $a\in\mathbb R^k$, such that $$\sigma_j\left\{s:\tfrac12e^{a_i}\le s_i\le2e^{a_i} \text{ for every }i\right\} \le C_{\mathrm{cell}}$$ for every sufficiently large $j$. The threshold for $j$ may depend on $a$.
  - Explanation: The mass of every normalized logarithmic cell is bounded by a constant independent of the cell position, using normalization, the cap identity and an interpolation of Hessian minors.
- **claim-uniform-balance** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-uniform-balance)): There is a constant $0<\rho\le1$ such that, for every $a\in\Omega$ and $t>0$, the compact convex body $$K_a(t)=\overline{S_u(a,t)}-a$$ satisfies $$\label{eq:uniform-section-balance} -\rho K_a(t)\subset K_a(t).$$ The same $\rho$ works for all base points and heights.
  - Explanation: Every tangent section is balanced about its base point with one universal ratio, because the epigraph is a one-direction model and centering is uniform.
- **claim-doubling-entire** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-doubling-entire)): The domain is $\Omega=\mathbb R^n$. Moreover, there are constants $\beta>1$ and $A>1$, depending only on $\rho$, such that every tangent-subtracted line restriction $$g(r)=u(a+re)-u(a)-r\,Du(a)\cdot e$$ satisfies $$\label{eq:gap-doubling} g(\beta r)\le A g(r)\qquad(r>0).$$ One may take $$\label{eq:doubling-constants} \beta=1+\frac{\rho}{2},\qquad A=\frac{(2+\rho)(1+\rho)}{\rho}.$$
  - Explanation: Uniform balance forces rays from any point to stay in the domain, so the domain is the whole space, and tangent gaps along lines satisfy a doubling inequality with explicit constants.
- **claim-normalized-modulus** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-normalized-modulus)): For all functions $v_t$ in <a href="#eq:normalized-sections" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-sections">[eq:normalized-sections]</a>, there is one positive function $b:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)$ such that $$\label{eq:normalized-modulus} S_{v_t}(x,b(r))\subset x+B_r \qquad\bigl(x\in B_{1/4},\ r>0\bigr).$$ In particular, with $\gamma=(4R)^{-1}$, $$\label{eq:uniform-strict-convexity} S_u(x,tb(r))\subset x+rS_u(0,t) \qquad \bigl(x\in\gamma S_u(0,t),\ r,t>0\bigr).$$
  - Explanation: Normalized sections admit a single modulus of strict convexity independent of height, base point in the stated range and direction.
- **claim-tw-interior** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-thm-tw-interior)): Let $O\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded convex domain and let $f\in C^4(O)$ have positive-definite Hessian and solve the affine maximal equation. Suppose $-1\le f\le0$. If a positive function $b_0$ gives the section control $$S_{f\|_O}(x,b_0(r))\subset B_r(x) \qquad(x\in O,\ r>0),$$ then $f\in C^\infty(O)$, and for each $O'\Subset O$ and integer $q\ge2$ there are positive constants $c,C_q$ such that $$D^2f\ge c\mathrm{Id},\qquad \|D^qf\|\le C_q \quad\hbox{on }O'.$$ The constants depend only on $n$, $\operatorname{diam}O$, $\operatorname{dist}(O',\partial O)$, and the specified lower modulus $b_0$, with additional dependence on $q$ for $C_q$.
  - Explanation: Given a prescribed lower modulus of convexity for sections, solutions of the affine maximal equation are smooth in the interior with a positive Hessian lower bound and derivative bounds depending only on the data.
- **claim-affine-complete** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-affine-complete)): Let $3\le n\le9$. Let $M$ be a smooth locally uniformly convex immersed hypersurface in $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ whose underlying manifold is connected and open (noncompact and without boundary). Suppose that $M$ is complete for the affine (Berwald–Blaschke) metric and is classically affine maximal (stationary for affine area under compactly supported smooth variations). Then $M$ is an invertible affine image of the standard elliptic paraboloid.
  - Explanation: An open, connected, affine-complete, classically affine maximal locally uniformly convex hypersurface in dimensions three through nine is an affine image of the elliptic paraboloid, by reduction to a complete graph.

        "exact_quote": "At every finite boundary point $b\\in\\partial\\Omega$, $$\\lim_{\\Omega\\ni x\\to b}u(x)=+\\infty.$$ The epigraph $$D=\\{(x,z):x\\in\\Omega,\\ z\\ge u(x)\\}$$ is a closed full-dimensional convex set with boundary equal to the graph of $u$.         "exact_quote": "Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href=\"#prop:centering\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:centering\">7</a>, any chosen positive fiber of any $k$-model can be moved to $s=\\mathbf1$ by positive diagonal changes in $s$ and normalized by an invertible linear change in $y$ so that $$\\overline B_{r_0}\\subset K_{\\mathbf1}\n \\subset\\overline B_{R_0},\n \\qquad r_0=\\frac2{C_*+1},\\quad R_0=2m.$$ For every fixed $0<a<1<b$, these normalized models satisfy $$\\label{eq:normalized-fiber-box}\n \\overline B_{ar_0}\\subset K_s\n \\subset\\overline B_{bR_0}\n \\qquad(s\\in[a,b]^k).$$ Their caps for fixed positive linear forms in $s$ and fixed positive top levels have uniform bounds, and they contain a fixed interior ball about $(\\mathbf1,0)$.*\n\n*For any sequence of affine images of $D$ converging to one of these models, comparable cap and fiber bounds hold eventually, uniformly on each fixed positive box.         "anchor": "stmt-prop-log-inequality",
        "exact_quote": "Let $3\\le n\\le9$ and $2\\le k\\le n$. On any tube satisfying the hypotheses of this section, there is a constant $C_n$, depending only on $n$, such that every $\\widetilde v\\in C_c^\\infty(\\log\\mathcal B)$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:log-inequality}\n \\int_{\\mathcal T}\\widetilde v(\\log s)^2\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm d\\sigma\n \\le C_n\\int_{\\mathcal T}\n       |\\nabla\\widetilde v(\\log s)|_{\\mathbb R^k}^2\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm d\\sigma.$$ Here $\\log s=(\\log s_1,\\ldots,\\log s_k)$ and the gradient on the right is Euclidean. No completeness of the metric $G$ is required.",

      },
      "evidence": [
        {

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **k-model** ([source](#stmt-def-model)): A *model with $k$ directions*, or a *$k$-model*, is a member $C$ of $\mathcal F(D)$ equipped with affine coordinates $$(s,y)\in\mathbb R^k\times\mathbb R^m,\qquad m=n+1-k, \qquad 1\le k\le n+1,$$ such that $$\{(s,0):s_i\ge0\}\subset C \subset\{(s,y):s_i\ge0\},$$ and every cap $C\cap\{\sum_i s_i\le T\}$, $T\ge0$, is compact. Its transverse fibers are $$K_s=\{y:(s,y)\in C\}.$$ The coordinates are part of the model data.
- **family F(D)** ([source](#sec:geometry)): Let $\mathcal F(D)$ be the family of all full-dimensional local limits of invertible affine images of $D$, including those images themselves.
- **inward conormal** ([source](#sec:area)): For a smooth locally strictly convex hypersurface parametrized by $X$, an *inward conormal* is a covector $\nu$ annihilating its tangent space and pointing into the convex body.

          "exact_quote": "A *model with $k$ directions*, or a *$k$-model*, is a member $C$ of $\\mathcal F(D)$ equipped with affine coordinates $$(s,y)\\in\\mathbb R^k\\times\\mathbb R^m,\\qquad m=n+1-k,\n \\qquad 1\\le k\\le n+1,$$ such that $$\\{(s,0):s_i\\ge0\\}\\subset C\n \\subset\\{(s,y):s_i\\ge0\\},$$ and every cap $C\\cap\\{\\sum_i s_i\\le T\\}$, $T\\ge0$, is compact. Its transverse fibers are $$K_s=\\{y:(s,y)\\in C\\}.$$ The coordinates are part of the model data.",

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-dimension-range | dimension n of the graph | ≤ | $3\le n\le9$ | integer dimension |
| q-affine-exponent | exponent delta of the affine area density | = | $\frac1{n+2}$ | depends on n only |
| q-coercivity-parameter | coercivity parameter lambda | = | $15/16$ | dimensions three through nine |
| q-determinant-lower-bound | determinant of the coefficient matrix at the chosen parameter | ≥ | $\frac7{128}$ | dimensions three through nine |
| q-inner-radius | inner radius of the normalized fiber | = | $\frac2{C_*+1}$ | depends on the centering constant |
| q-outer-radius | outer radius of the normalized fiber | = | $2m$ | depends on the transverse dimension |
| q-doubling-beta | dilation factor beta | = | $1+\frac{\rho}{2}$ | depends on the balance constant |
| q-doubling-amplitude | amplitude factor A | = | $\frac{(2+\rho)(1+\rho)}{\rho}$ | depends on the balance constant |
| q-section-contraction | contraction factor gamma | = | $(4R)^{-1}$ | depends on the normalization radius |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-proper-sections** [claim](#stmt-lem-proper-sections) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-proper-sections): Argue by contradiction with a finite-length lifted curve, then use convexity along rays. -> boundary blow-up, closed epigraph, compact caps
- **step-cap-compactness** [claim](#stmt-lem-cap-compactness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-cap-compactness): Escaping points give a direction in the limit cap; then Hausdorff inclusions. -> uniform boundedness and Hausdorff convergence of caps
- **step-model-fibers** [claim](#stmt-lem-model-fibers) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-model-fibers): Recession by limits of convex combinations, contraction toward the origin, interior ball slices. -> monotone compact full-dimensional fibers; a one-direction model exists
- **step-linear-normalization** [claim](#stmt-lem-linear-normalization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-linear-normalization): Maximum-volume inscribed ellipsoid and a convex combination of two balls. -> inner and outer ball normalization
- **step-centering** [claim](#stmt-prop-centering) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-centering): Contradiction: normalize asymmetric fibers, take a limit, rescale a thin slice to build a model with one more direction. -> uniform centering constant
- **step-normalized-fibers** [claim](#stmt-cor-normalized-fibers) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-normalized-fibers): Monotonicity and contraction on boxes, transfer of cap bounds to approximants. -> fiber and cap bounds on positive boxes
- **step-stationarity** [claim](#stmt-lem-stationarity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-stationarity): Divergence-free cofactor and first variation, partition of unity, affine covariance of area. -> stationarity of affine area for all affine images
- **step-area-bound** [claim](#stmt-lem-area-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-area-bound): Injective Gauss map, projection bound for Euclidean area, Hölder inequality. -> affine area bound in a ball
- **step-cap-identity** [claim](#stmt-lem-cap-identity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-cap-identity): Test stationarity with a product of a support-type function and a cutoff, integrate by parts. -> cap identity
- **step-log-inequality** [claim](#stmt-prop-log-inequality) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-log-inequality): Support coordinates, weighted integral identities, coercive quadratic form, Young and Cauchy–Schwarz inequalities. -> logarithmic Poincaré-type inequality
- **step-positive-cap-area** [claim](#stmt-lem-positive-cap-area) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-positive-cap-area): Concavity of the area integrand compared against a quadratic perturbation, with stationarity. -> positive lower bound on cap area
- **step-log-cell-bound** [claim](#stmt-lem-log-cell-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-log-cell-bound): Normalize by diagonal and linear maps, apply the cap identity with convex test functions, Hessian minor bounds and Hölder interpolation. -> uniform bound on each logarithmic cell
- **step-one-direction** [claim](#stmt-prop-one-direction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-log-cell-bound-2): Compare polynomial growth from cell bounds with exponential growth from iterating the logarithmic inequality. -> maximal number of directions is one
- **step-uniform-balance** [claim](#stmt-lem-uniform-balance) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-uniform-balance): View the epigraph as a one-direction model and apply uniform centering. -> uniform balance of tangent sections
- **step-doubling-entire** [claim](#stmt-lem-doubling-entire) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-doubling-entire): Balance of sections gives extension of rays and a doubling inequality by convexity. -> entire domain and gap doubling
- **step-normalized-modulus** [claim](#stmt-lem-normalized-modulus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-normalized-modulus): Fixed-radius gap from balance, iterate doubling to define an explicit modulus. -> uniform modulus of strict convexity
- **step-tw-interior** [claim](#stmt-thm-tw-interior) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-tw-interior): Cited interior estimate for affine maximal solutions with a prescribed convexity modulus. -> interior smoothness and derivative bounds
- **step-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-tw-interior): Rescale sections, apply the interior estimate uniformly, show the third derivative vanishes. -> quadratic graph on the whole space
- **step-affine-complete** [claim](#stmt-cor-affine-complete) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-affine-complete): Use cited results to pass to a complete graph, preserve stationarity under the affine change, apply the main theorem. -> affine image of the elliptic paraboloid

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-dimension-window**: The rigidity conclusion is asserted only in the dimension window of Theorem 1; the coercivity estimate behind it is what restricts the dimension.
- **inv-smooth-convex-solution**: The solution must be smooth with positive-definite Hessian and satisfy the affine maximal equation.
- **inv-euclidean-completeness**: Under Euclidean completeness of the graph, the domain is the whole space and the function is a quadratic with positive-definite matrix, so the graph is an affine image of the standard paraboloid.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-high-dimension**: Apply the rigidity theorem in dimensions outside the stated window. -> The proof relies on a quadratic coercivity estimate whose strict positivity is available only inside the window, so the conclusion is not covered outside it.
- **anti-other-exponents**: Transfer the rigidity to affine maximal type equations with a different determinant power. -> Nonquadratic Euclidean-complete examples are known for other exponents, so rigidity can fail.
- **anti-completeness-kind**: Treat Euclidean completeness and affine completeness as interchangeable hypotheses. -> They are different conditions, so a theorem proved under one is not automatically available under the other.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main result is a rigidity statement for smooth entire solutions, proved by compactness limits, contradiction arguments and interior regularity estimates. It gives no finite procedure whose output could be computed and checked: the statement asserts that if the graph is complete for the induced Euclidean metric then the domain is the whole space and the function is quadratic, which is qualitative and existential in nature.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves a rigidity statement about smooth affine maximal graphs. It specifies no program, interface or data format that software could implement. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result concerns exact classification of smooth entire solutions of a fourth-order equation. It gives no discretisation, time stepping or physical model that a simulation could use. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No procedure with inputs and outputs is given, so no verification or analysis tool can be built from the statements. The proofs are existence and contradiction arguments. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The main argument is by contradiction between polynomial and exponential growth of a measure on smooth approximants. It is not a finite constructive procedure, so there is no complexity or pseudocode to state. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper introduces geometric objects such as models, fibers and support functions. None is a storable structure with operations and cost guarantees. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no computation, dataflow or arithmetic kernel in the paper, so nothing maps onto accelerator hardware. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No hardness assumption, primitive or adversary appears. The subject is differential geometry of convex hypersurfaces. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no data, loss or learning model. Its hypotheses are smoothness, convexity and completeness, which do not describe a trainable hypothesis class. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Affine area is the functional whose critical points are studied, but the paper only classifies stationary graphs and gives no minimisation method, rate or convergence claim. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There are no agents, messages or consensus notions. The analysis is local geometry plus global compactness of one convex body. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The natural home is pure analysis and geometry, namely a rigidity theorem for fully nonlinear equations. It is not an engineering contract; its interior estimate is only a regularity statement with no specified solver. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-dimension-window** (hypothesis): The rigidity theorem and the logarithmic inequality are proved only for the dimension window of the main theorem; the proof's coercivity estimate has strictly positive determinant only inside it. Blocked use: ['Applying the rigidity conclusion to affine maximal graphs of dimension ten or above', 'Extending the logarithmic inequality to dimensions outside the stated range']
- **limit-euclidean-completeness** (hypothesis): The graph theorem requires completeness for the induced Euclidean metric; affine completeness is a different hypothesis and is handled only through the separate corollary for hypersurfaces. Blocked use: ['Using the graph theorem for graphs complete only in some other sense', 'Treating Euclidean and affine completeness as interchangeable']
- **limit-smoothness-classical** (hypothesis): The solution must be a smooth function with positive-definite Hessian satisfying the classical affine maximal equation; weak or singular solutions are not covered. Blocked use: ['Applying the theorem to singular or weak entire solutions', 'Applying it to graphs whose Hessian is only semidefinite']
- **limit-other-exponents** (counterexample_scope): For determinant-power equations with other exponents, nonquadratic Euclidean-complete examples exist, so rigidity is specific to the classical exponent. Blocked use: ['Transferring the rigidity theorem to affine maximal type equations with a different determinant power']
- **limit-nonconstructive-constants** (nonconstructive): The uniform centering constant and the exclusion of several directions come from contradiction and compactness arguments, so no computable values of these constants are given. Blocked use: ['Quoting a numerical value for the centering constant', 'Using the argument as an algorithm']
- **limit-corollary-hypotheses** (hypothesis): The hypersurface corollary needs a connected, open, smooth, locally uniformly convex immersed hypersurface that is affine complete and classically affine maximal. Blocked use: ['Applying the corollary to compact or bounded hypersurfaces', 'Applying it without affine completeness']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 353 (`lean/docs/353.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [The affine Bernstein theorem in dimensions three through nine](../../preprints/The-affine-Bernstein-theorem-in-dimensions-three-through-nine-September-24-2026/main.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization proves the Euclidean-complete affine Bernstein theorem for graph dimensions $3\le n\le9$. If a smooth function on a nonempty open convex domain has positive-definite Hessian, satisfies the affine maximal equation, and its graph is complete in the induced Euclidean metric, then the domain is all of $\mathbb R^n$ and the function is a positive-definite quadratic polynomial plus an affine term. Its graph is therefore an elliptic paraboloid.
>
> The paper's extension to immersed hypersurfaces without an initial graph assumption is outside this selected statement.

| `affineWeight` | def |
| `AffineMaximalOn` | def |
| `graphPathLength` | def |
| `graphEDist` | def |
| `EuclideanGraphComplete` | def |

Comparator source `AffineBernstein.lean` (4066 bytes, sha256 `67061d78f2ae07474c835198ff5b19f41ac3ba4e06d9c6f03443ef68fbc9d2bf`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

open Set MeasureTheory
open scoped BigOperators ContDiff ENNReal

namespace AffineBernstein

abbrev Space (n : ℕ) := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n)

def coordinateVector (n : ℕ) (i : Fin n) : Space n :=
  EuclideanSpace.single i 1

/-- Coordinate Hessian. On the open domain it depends only on the restriction of `u`.
The order of the two differentiations is immaterial under the smoothness hypothesis. -/
def hessian {n : ℕ} (u : Space n → ℝ) (x : Space n) : Matrix (Fin n) (Fin n) ℝ :=
  fun i j => fderiv ℝ (fun y => fderiv ℝ u y (coordinateVector n j)) x
    (coordinateVector n i)

/-- The coefficient U^{ij} = det(D²u) (D²u)^{-1}_{ij}. -/
def cofactorHessian {n : ℕ} (u : Space n → ℝ) (x : Space n) :
    Matrix (Fin n) (Fin n) ℝ :=
  (hessian u x).det • (hessian u x)⁻¹

/-- The classical determinant weight; in particular, this is not a generalized exponent. -/
def affineWeight {n : ℕ} (u : Space n → ℝ) (x : Space n) : ℝ :=
  Real.rpow (hessian u x).det (-(((n : ℝ) + 1) / ((n : ℝ) + 2)))

def AffineMaximalOn {n : ℕ} (Ω : Set (Space n)) (u : Space n → ℝ) : Prop :=
  ∀ x ∈ Ω, ∑ i : Fin n, ∑ j : Fin n,
    cofactorHessian u x i j * hessian (affineWeight u) x i j = 0

/-- Length on [0,1] for g_x(v,v) = ‖v‖² + (Du_x v)², the metric induced
by the Euclidean graph embedding x ↦ (x,u(x)). The product norm on Lean's ordinary
product type is NOT used (it would be the maximum norm). -/
def graphPathLength {n : ℕ} (u : Space n → ℝ) (γ : ℝ → Space n) : ℝ≥0∞ :=
  ∫⁻ t in Icc (0 : ℝ) 1,
    ENNReal.ofReal (Real.sqrt
      (‖derivWithin γ (Icc (0 : ℝ) 1) t‖ ^ 2 +
       (fderiv ℝ u (γ t) (derivWithin γ (Icc (0 : ℝ) 1) t)) ^ 2))

/-- The intrinsic extended distance: infimum of lengths of C¹ paths in the domain.
Using a single C¹ path gives the same intrinsic distance as piecewise C¹ paths,
by smooth endpoint reparameterization and concatenation. -/
def graphEDist {n : ℕ} (Ω : Set (Space n)) (u : Space n → ℝ)
    (x y : Space n) : ℝ≥0∞ :=
  ⨅ (γ : ℝ → Space n)
    (_ : ContDiffOn ℝ 1 γ (Icc (0 : ℝ) 1))
    (_ : MapsTo γ (Icc (0 : ℝ) 1) Ω)
    (_ : γ 0 = x) (_ : γ 1 = y), graphPathLength u γ

/-- Sequential completeness for the induced Euclidean path metric. This states
that every intrinsic Cauchy sequence of points in Ω has an intrinsic limit in Ω.
It imposes no growth condition and no completeness condition on the affine metric. -/
def EuclideanGraphComplete {n : ℕ} (Ω : Set (Space n)) (u : Space n → ℝ) : Prop :=
  ∀ x : ℕ → Space n, (∀ i, x i ∈ Ω) →
    (∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ N : ℕ, ∀ i ≥ N, ∀ j ≥ N,
      graphEDist Ω u (x i) (x j) < ENNReal.ofReal ε) →
    ∃ a ∈ Ω, ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ N : ℕ, ∀ i ≥ N,
      graphEDist Ω u (x i) a < ENNReal.ofReal ε

/-- The graph, regarded as an affine subset of R^{n+1}. -/
def graph {n : ℕ} (Ω : Set (Space n)) (u : Space n → ℝ) : Set (Space n × ℝ) :=
  {p | p.1 ∈ Ω ∧ p.2 = u p.1}

def standardParaboloid (n : ℕ) : Set (Space n × ℝ) :=
  {p | p.2 = ∑ i : Fin n, (p.1 i) ^ 2}

/-- Literal main theorem, with its stated affine-image consequence. -/
theorem affine_bernstein
    (n : ℕ) (hn₃ : 3 ≤ n) (hn₉ : n ≤ 9)
    (Ω : Set (Space n)) (hΩne : Ω.Nonempty) (hΩopen : IsOpen Ω)
    (hΩconvex : Convex ℝ Ω) (u : Space n → ℝ)
    (hu : ContDiffOn ℝ ∞ u Ω)
    (hpos : ∀ x ∈ Ω, (hessian u x).PosDef)
    (hmax : AffineMaximalOn Ω u)
    (hcomplete : EuclideanGraphComplete Ω u) :
    Ω = univ ∧
      (∃ (A : Matrix (Fin n) (Fin n) ℝ) (b : Space n) (c : ℝ),
        A.IsSymm ∧ A.PosDef ∧
        ∀ x, u x = (1 / 2 : ℝ) * (∑ i : Fin n, ∑ j : Fin n, x i * A i j * x j)
          + (∑ i : Fin n, b i * x i) + c) ∧
      (∃ e : (Space n × ℝ) ≃ᵃ[ℝ] (Space n × ℝ),
        e '' standardParaboloid n = graph Ω u) := by
  sorry

end AffineBernstein

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the Euclidean-complete affine Bernstein conjecture in dimensions three through nine: a smooth locally uniformly convex affine maximal graph is an elliptic paraboloid whenever its induced Euclidean metric is complete. The same conclusion holds, without an initial graph assumption, for connected smooth open (noncompact and without boundary) affine-complete locally uniformly convex immersed hypersurfaces that are classically affine maximal in these dimensions.

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

The affine Bernstein problem asks whether a complete locally uniformly convex affine maximal hypersurface must be an elliptic paraboloid. The meaning of completeness is essential: completeness for the metric induced from Euclidean space and completeness for the affine metric are different hypotheses. We prove the Euclidean-complete graph assertion in dimensions three through nine.

<a id="eq:affine-maximal"></a>
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a nonempty open convex domain, and let $u\in C^\infty(\Omega)$ have positive-definite Hessian. Write $$U^{ij}=\det(D^2u)(D^2u)^{-1}_{ij},
 \qquad
 w=(\det D^2u)^{-(n+1)/(n+2)}.$$ The affine maximal equation is $$\label{eq:affine-maximal}
 U^{ij}w_{ij}=0.$$ It is the Euler equation for affine area $\int(\det D^2u)^{1/(n+2)}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dx$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Let $3\le n\le9$. Suppose $u\in C^\infty(\Omega)$ has positive-definite Hessian and satisfies <a href="#eq:affine-maximal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-maximal">[eq:affine-maximal]</a>. If its graph is complete for the induced Euclidean metric $$g_{ij}=\delta_{ij}+u_i u_j,$$ then $\Omega=\mathbb R^n$ and $$u(x)=\tfrac12 x^{\mathsf T}Ax+b\cdot x+c$$ for a positive-definite symmetric matrix $A$, a vector $b$, and a constant $c$. In particular, the graph is an invertible affine image of the standard elliptic paraboloid.*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> resolves the Euclidean-complete affine Bernstein conjecture for smooth locally uniformly convex graphs in dimensions three through nine. It includes the entire graph assertion and imposes neither a growth bound nor completeness of the affine metric.

Chern asked whether an entire locally uniformly convex affine maximal surface must be an elliptic paraboloid (Chern 1979) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Chern1979](#ref-Chern1979)]. Calabi developed the affine variational framework and proved the surface conclusion under both Euclidean and affine completeness (Calabi 1982) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Calabi1982](#ref-Calabi1982)]; see also (Trudinger and Wang 2002, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[TW2002](#ref-TW2002)]. For a graph, the affine (Berwald–Blaschke) metric is $$g^{\mathrm{aff}}_{ij}=(\det D^2u)^{-1/(n+2)}u_{ij},$$ so its completeness is a different condition from that in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Trudinger and Wang proved the Euclidean-complete surface theorem, dispensing with affine completeness (Trudinger and Wang 2000, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[TW2000](#ref-TW2000)]. Li and Jia gave a separate proof of the surface theorem under affine completeness alone (Li and Jia 2001) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[LiJia2001](#ref-LiJia2001)].

The analytic reduction in higher dimensions is also due to Trudinger and Wang. Their interior estimates and rescaling argument prove rigidity for Euclidean-complete graphs in every dimension provided the separation of the graph from its tangent planes has a modulus of strict convexity that remains uniform under normalization of tangent-plane sublevel sets (Trudinger and Wang 2000, Theorem 4.2 and Corollary 4.3) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[TW2000](#ref-TW2000)]. They establish this uniform control in dimension two by studying convex limits and their supporting contact sets (Trudinger and Wang 2000, sec. 5) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[TW2000](#ref-TW2000)]. Obtaining it in higher dimensions without an additional hypothesis is the obstacle addressed here. Later work has developed alternative analytic approaches: Wang and Zhou give a proof of the entire-surface theorem using the partial Legendre transform, and higher-dimensional rigidity results under integral bounds on the Hessian and the inverse Hessian determinant (Wang and Zhou 2023, Theorem 1.2 and Corollary 1.6) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[WangZhou2023](#ref-WangZhou2023)]. Our proof instead derives the required convexity control from the geometry of affine limits, with no growth or integral bound assumed.

For locally uniformly convex hypersurfaces in dimension at least two, Trudinger and Wang proved that affine completeness implies Euclidean completeness (Trudinger and Wang 2002, Theorem A) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[TW2002](#ref-TW2002)]; the converse fails in general. Their global graph theorem (Trudinger and Wang 2002, Corollary 1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[TW2002](#ref-TW2002)] therefore extends our conclusion to connected smooth open affine-complete locally uniformly convex immersed hypersurfaces that are classically affine maximal, without an initial graph assumption. The precise statement and proof appear in Corollary <a href="#cor:affine-complete" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:affine-complete">21</a>.

The singular entire affine maximal graph constructed by Trudinger and Wang in dimension ten (Trudinger and Wang 2000, sec. 7) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[TW2000](#ref-TW2000)] is a weak solution. It is not smooth and does not furnish a counterexample under the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Our argument identifies the dimension restriction in a quadratic coercivity estimate, whose strict positivity holds through dimension nine.

The distinction between the classical equation and its determinant-power generalizations is also relevant. For affine maximal type equations with $w=(\det D^2u)^a$, Sun, Xing and Xu construct nonquadratic Euclidean-complete examples in every dimension $n\ge2$ for $a\in[-n/(n+1),0)$ (Sun, Xing, and Xu 2026, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[SunXingXu2026](#ref-SunXingXu2026)]. This interval excludes the classical exponent $-(n+1)/(n+2)$ in Equation <a href="#eq:affine-maximal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-maximal">[eq:affine-maximal]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The main ideas

For $a\in\Omega$, let $$l_a(x)=u(a)+Du(a)\cdot(x-a),\qquad
 S_u(a,t)=\{x\in\Omega:u(x)<l_a(x)+t\}\quad(t>0).$$ These tangent sections measure the separation of the graph from its supporting planes. The geometric objective is uniform balance: for one $\rho>0$, reflection about the base point followed by contraction by $\rho$ takes every closed section into itself. This control must hold at every base point and every height.

We obtain balance by studying full-dimensional closed convex limits of affine images of the epigraph $D=\{(x,z):x\in\Omega,\ z\ge u(x)\}$. Write the coordinates of such a limit as $(s,y)\in\mathbb R^k\times\mathbb R^{n+1-k}$. A model with $k$ directions is supported in $s_i\ge0$, contains $\{(s,0):s_i\ge0\}$, and has compact caps cut out by $\sum_i s_i\le T$. Its transverse fibers are $K_s=\{y:(s,y)\in D_{\mathrm{lim}}\}$, where $D_{\mathrm{lim}}$ denotes the limiting body. Maximizing $k$ forces a uniform centering estimate $-K_s\subset C_*K_s$ for all positive fibers: failure of this estimate produces an additional nonnegative direction by a further affine rescaling. Normalized caps and convex limits also play a central role in the two-dimensional argument of Trudinger and Wang (Trudinger and Wang 2000, sec. 5) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[TW2000](#ref-TW2000)] <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[TW2000](#ref-TW2000)]; here the rescaling is organized by the maximal number of nonnegative directions.

The analytic step excludes $k\ge2$. Support functions of the fibers give a positive second form and a natural invariant measure. The affine maximal equation yields an integral inequality in logarithmic coordinates, forcing exponential growth of a related measure. A cap identity and estimates for Hessian minors bound its mass on each normalized log cell, allowing only polynomial growth. Both estimates are used on smooth approximants over one sufficiently large finite box; the limiting body needs no regularity.

These two ingredients—increasing the number of model directions when centering degenerates, and combining a logarithmic integral inequality with affine-invariant cell bounds—are the principal technical contributions. The resulting uniform balance of tangent sections supplies a scale-independent modulus of strict convexity. We then use the interior estimates and section-rescaling argument of Trudinger and Wang (Trudinger and Wang 2000, sec. 2 and 4): normalized third derivatives stay bounded, while transforming them back gives a bound tending to zero. John’s ellipsoid theorem (John 1948; Ball 1992) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[John1948](#ref-John1948), [Ball1992](#ref-Ball1992)] supplies the normalizations. The compactness arguments and the cap and tube estimates used here are proved below.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Notation and organization

Closed sections use the corresponding non-strict inequality. The symbol $B_r(x)$ denotes an open Euclidean ball, and $B_r=B_r(0)$; its ambient dimension will be clear. The notation $H^{-1}$ for a positive second form specifies the dual norm on covectors. All constants in uniform estimates may depend on the fixed dimension and, where stated, on the family of affine limits of the given epigraph.

Section <a href="#sec:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry">2</a> establishes the compactness and centering argument. Sections <a href="#sec:area" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:area">3</a> and <a href="#sec:tubes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tubes">4</a> derive the cap identity and the tube inequality. Section <a href="#sec:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:bounds">5</a> excludes models with several directions, and Section <a href="#sec:rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rigidity">6</a> deduces section control and proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="sec:geometry"></a>
### Convex limits and centered fibers

The objective of this section is to show that fibers are uniformly centered once the number of model directions is maximal. We first obtain compact caps from graph completeness, then normalize a hypothetical sequence of increasingly asymmetric fibers. Its limit supplies the additional direction that contradicts maximality.

Throughout this section, $n\ge2$, $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ is nonempty, open and convex, and $u\in C^\infty(\Omega)$ has positive-definite Hessian. We assume only that its graph is complete for the induced Euclidean metric. No differential equation is needed in this section. For $a\in\Omega$, write $$l_a(x)=u(a)+Du(a)\cdot(x-a),
 \qquad g_a(x)=u(x)-l_a(x).$$

<a id="stmt-lem-proper-sections"></a>
<div id="lem:proper-sections" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2**. *At every finite boundary point $b\in\partial\Omega$, $$\lim_{\Omega\ni x\to b}u(x)=+\infty.$$ The epigraph $$D=\{(x,z):x\in\Omega,\ z\ge u(x)\}$$ is a closed full-dimensional convex set with boundary equal to the graph of $u$. For every $a\in\Omega$ and $t\ge0$, the set $\{x\in\Omega:g_a(x)\le t\}$ is compact and contained in $\Omega$. Consequently the cap $D\cap\{z-l_a(x)\le t\}$ is compact.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-proper-sections"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-proper-sections</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose $x_j\to b\in\partial\Omega$ and $u(x_j)\le A$. Fix $a\in\Omega$. Convexity, followed by continuity at the interior point $a+q(b-a)$, gives $$u(a+q(b-a))\le (1-q)u(a)+qA,\qquad 0\le q<1.$$ An affine supporting function at $a$ gives a lower bound on the same segment. The resulting bounded convex function of $q$ has finite total variation and a finite limit as $q\uparrow1$. Its lifted curve has finite Euclidean length, since $$\int_0^1\sqrt{|b-a|^2+
       \left|\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm d}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm dq}u(a+q(b-a))\right|^2}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dq
 \le |b-a|+\int_0^1
       \left|\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm d}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm dq}u(a+q(b-a))\right|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dq<\infty.$$ Its tail is therefore Cauchy in the graph metric, but its base coordinate tends to $b\notin\Omega$. This contradicts completeness. The asserted boundary limit follows. It also shows that a bounded convergent sequence of points of $D$ cannot have its base limit on $\partial\Omega$. Thus $D$ is closed, and its boundary is exactly the graph.

Choose $r,c>0$ such that $\overline B_r(a)\subset\Omega$ and $D^2u\ge c\mathrm{Id}$ on this ball. Along every ray from $a$, convexity of $g_a$, together with $g_a(a)=0$, gives $$g_a(x)\ge \frac{cr}{2}|x-a|
 \qquad (x\in\Omega,\ |x-a|\ge r).$$ Indeed, $g_a\ge cr^2/2$ at the point of the ray at distance $r$, and its quotient by the distance is nondecreasing. Thus each stated sublevel is bounded. The boundary limit just proved makes it closed in $\mathbb R^n$ and contained in $\Omega$. On such a compact base, both $l_a$ and the cap height are bounded, which proves the last claim. ◻

</details>

We use local convergence of nonempty closed convex sets in the sense of locally uniform convergence of their distance functions. This topology has the following elementary properties. Every point of a limit is approximated by points of the approximating sets, and every limit of a convergent sequence of such points belongs to the limit. If the limit has nonempty interior, every compact subset of its interior is eventually contained in the interiors of the approximants. For the latter statement, enclose a point in a simplex whose vertices lie in the limit, approximate its vertices, and then use a finite cover of the compact subset. A sequence of closed convex sets meeting a fixed bounded set has a locally convergent subsequence: their distance functions are locally equibounded and $1$-Lipschitz, and a diagonal application of compactness gives the distance function of a nonempty closed convex set.

Let $\mathcal F(D)$ be the family of all full-dimensional local limits of invertible affine images of $D$, including those images themselves. The family is invariant under invertible affine maps and closed under further full-dimensional local limits. To justify the last assertion explicitly, the distance-function topology is metrizable by $$d_{\mathrm{loc}}(C,E)=
 \sum_{q=1}^{\infty}2^{-q}
 \min\left\{1,\sup_{|X|\le q}
       |\operatorname{dist}(X,C)-\operatorname{dist}(X,E)|\right\}.$$ If $C_j\in\mathcal F(D)$ tends to a full-dimensional set $C$, choose an affine image $E_j$ of $D$ with $d_{\mathrm{loc}}(E_j,C_j)<1/j$. Then $E_j\to C$. Affine invariance follows because a fixed invertible affine map and its inverse take bounded sets to bounded sets and preserve the two pointwise convergence properties above. Thus affine maps varying with $j$ may be applied before this diagonal choice.

<a id="stmt-lem-cap-compactness"></a>
<div id="lem:cap-compactness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3**. *Suppose $C_j\to C$ locally, and let $\ell$ be an affine function. If $C\cap\{\ell\le b\}$ is bounded and contains a point $q$ with $\ell(q)<b$, then the caps $C_j\cap\{\ell\le b\}$ are eventually uniformly bounded. They converge in Hausdorff distance to $C\cap\{\ell\le b\}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cap-compactness"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cap-compactness</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $q_j\in C_j$ with $q_j\to q$, so $\ell(q_j)<b$ eventually. If points $p_j\in C_j\cap\{\ell\le b\}$ escape every bounded set, pass to a subsequence for which $$\frac{p_j-q_j}{|p_j-q_j|}\longrightarrow v,\qquad |v|=1.$$ For each fixed $t\ge0$, the points $q_j+t(p_j-q_j)/|p_j-q_j|$ eventually lie on the segments $[q_j,p_j]$. Their limit $q+tv$ therefore belongs to $C\cap\{\ell\le b\}$, contradicting boundedness.

Uniform boundedness and local convergence show that every limit point of the approximating caps lies in the limit cap. Conversely, if $x$ belongs to the limit cap, then $(1-\eta)x+\eta q$ lies strictly below the top for every $0<\eta\le1$ and is approximated by points of the approximating caps. Letting $\eta\downarrow0$ and using compactness gives the reverse Hausdorff inclusion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-def-model"></a>
<div id="def:model" class="definition">

**Definition 4**. A *model with $k$ directions*, or a *$k$-model*, is a member $C$ of $\mathcal F(D)$ equipped with affine coordinates $$(s,y)\in\mathbb R^k\times\mathbb R^m,\qquad m=n+1-k,
 \qquad 1\le k\le n+1,$$ such that $$\{(s,0):s_i\ge0\}\subset C
 \subset\{(s,y):s_i\ge0\},$$ and every cap $C\cap\{\sum_i s_i\le T\}$, $T\ge0$, is compact. Its transverse fibers are $$K_s=\{y:(s,y)\in C\}.$$ The coordinates are part of the model data.

</div>

<a id="stmt-lem-model-fibers"></a>
<div id="lem:model-fibers" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:fiber-monotonicity"></a>
**Lemma 5**. *For a $k$-model, addition of any vector $(v,0)$ with $v_i\ge0$ preserves $C$. Every fiber $K_s$ with $s_i>0$ is compact, full-dimensional in $\mathbb R^m$, and contains zero. Moreover, $$\label{eq:fiber-monotonicity}
 K_s\subset K_t\quad(s_i\le t_i),
 \qquad K_{\lambda s}\subset\lambda K_s\quad(\lambda\ge1).$$ At least one $1$-model exists.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-model-fibers"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-model-fibers</summary>

*Proof.* The recession assertion holds for any closed convex set containing the indicated orthant, without a full-dimensionality assumption. For $X\in C$ and $v_i\ge0$, take the limit as $\eta\downarrow0$ of $$(1-\eta)X+\eta(v/\eta,0)\in C$$ to obtain $X+(v,0)\in C$. This proves fiber monotonicity. Since the ambient origin belongs to $C$, contraction toward it proves the second inclusion in <a href="#eq:fiber-monotonicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fiber-monotonicity">[eq:fiber-monotonicity]</a>. Containment of zero and compactness follow from the definition.

Take an interior ball of $C$ centered at $(s^0,y^0)$; all coordinates of $s^0$ are positive. Given $s_i>0$, choose $0<\eta\le1$ with $\eta s^0_i\le s_i$. Contract the transverse slice of this ball by $\eta$ about the ambient origin, and increase the $s$ coordinates to $s$. The resulting transverse ball lies in $K_s$, proving full dimension. When $m=0$ this assertion uses the usual convention for $\mathbb R^0$.

Finally, for any $a\in\Omega$, the coordinates $s=z-l_a(x)$ and $y=x-a$ make $D$ a $1$-model by Lemma <a href="#lem:proper-sections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:proper-sections">2</a>. ◻

</details>

We shall use the following precise consequence of the ellipsoid theorem of John (John 1948) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[John1948](#ref-John1948)]; see also the contact-point formulation and nonsymmetric outer-radius deduction in (Ball 1992, sec. 0) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Ball1992](#ref-Ball1992)]. The normalization is linear and therefore preserves the distinguished origin.

<a id="stmt-lem-linear-normalization"></a>
<div id="lem:linear-normalization" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:linear-normalization"></a>
<a id="eq:centered-normalization"></a>
**Lemma 6**. *Let $K\subset\mathbb R^d$, $d\ge1$, be compact and convex with nonempty interior and $0\in K$. There are an invertible linear map $A$ and a vector $c$ such that $$\label{eq:linear-normalization}
 \overline B_1(c)\subset AK\subset\overline B_d(c),
 \qquad |c|\le d.$$ In particular $AK\subset\overline B_{2d}$; the origin is allowed to lie on $\partial K$. If in addition $-K\subset C_*K$ for some $C_*\ge1$, then $$\label{eq:centered-normalization}
 \overline B_{2/(C_*+1)}
 \subset AK\subset\overline B_{2d}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-linear-normalization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-linear-normalization</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a maximum-volume inscribed ellipsoid and take the linear part of its normalization to a unit ball. John’s Theorem gives the two inclusions in <a href="#eq:linear-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:linear-normalization">[eq:linear-normalization]</a>. Since $0\in AK$, the outer inclusion implies $|c|\le d$. The centering assumption also gives $\overline B_{1/C_*}(-c/C_*)\subset AK$. The convex combination of this ball and $\overline B_1(c)$ with respective weights $C_*/(C_*+1)$ and $1/(C_*+1)$ is the centered ball in <a href="#eq:centered-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:centered-normalization">[eq:centered-normalization]</a>. ◻

</details>

By Lemma <a href="#lem:model-fibers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:model-fibers">5</a>, the set of direction numbers achieved by models is a nonempty subset of $\{1,\ldots,n+1\}$. Let $k$ be its maximum.

<a id="stmt-prop-centering"></a>
<div id="prop:centering" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:model-centering"></a>
**Proposition 7** (Uniform centering at maximal direction number). *Suppose $m=n+1-k>0$. There is a constant $C_*\ge1$, depending on the family $\mathcal F(D)$, such that $$\label{eq:model-centering}
 -K_s\subset C_*K_s
 \qquad(s_i>0)$$ for every $k$-model and every admissible choice of its coordinates. In particular, zero is an interior point of every such fiber.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-centering"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-centering</summary>

<a id="eq:asymmetric-normalization"></a>
*Proof.* Suppose that no uniform constant exists. Choose $k$-models and positive fibers for which the inclusion with constant $j$ fails. Positive diagonal changes in $s$ put those fibers at $\mathbf1=(1,\ldots,1)$, and Lemma <a href="#lem:linear-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:linear-normalization">6</a> provides linear changes in $y$. Denote the resulting models by $C_j$ and their fibers at $\mathbf1$ by $F_j$. Thus $$\label{eq:asymmetric-normalization}
 \overline B_1(c_j)\subset F_j\subset\overline B_{2m},
 \qquad |c_j|\le m,
 \qquad -F_j\not\subset jF_j.$$ All these coordinate changes preserve the model properties and the failed inclusion.

<a id="eq:normalized-cap-bound"></a>
For $T\ge1$ and $0\le s_i\le T$, Lemma <a href="#lem:model-fibers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:model-fibers">5</a> gives $$\label{eq:normalized-cap-bound}
 K_s^{(j)}\subset K_{T\mathbf1}^{(j)}
 \subset T F_j\subset\overline B_{2mT}.$$ Pass to a local limit $C$ and a subsequence with $c_j\to c$. The limit is contained in the nonnegative $s$ halfspaces, contains the entire orthant at $y=0$, and has compact caps by <a href="#eq:normalized-cap-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-cap-bound">[eq:normalized-cap-bound]</a>. Moreover, $$[1,2]^k\times\overline B_1(c_j)\subset C_j,$$ so its limit has nonempty interior. The closure properties of $\mathcal F(D)$ show that $C$ is a $k$-model.

Its fiber $F=K_{\mathbf1}$ is the Hausdorff limit of $F_j$. Only one inclusion needs explanation. If $(s^{(j)},y_j)\in C_j$ tends to $(\mathbf1,y)\in C$, let $$a_j=\min\left\{1,\frac1{s^{(j)}_1},\ldots,
                         \frac1{s^{(j)}_k}\right\}.$$ The denominators are positive for large $j$, and $a_j\to1$. Contracting by $a_j$ and then increasing the $s$ coordinates gives $(\mathbf1,a_jy_j)\in C_j$. Thus every point of $F$ is approximated from the exact fibers $F_j$; the other inclusion follows from local convergence. Uniform boundedness makes this Hausdorff convergence. If zero were interior to $F$, the $F_j$ would eventually contain a fixed ball centered at zero. Their common outer bound would then give a fixed centering constant, contrary to <a href="#eq:asymmetric-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:asymmetric-normalization">[eq:asymmetric-normalization]</a>. Hence $0\in\partial F$.

A supporting linear functional of $F$ at zero can be made the coordinate $y_1$, with $F\subset\{y_1\ge0\}$. This inequality holds on all of $C$: contract any $(s,y)\in C$ until each $s_i\le1$, then increase the $s$ coordinates to $\mathbf1$ and apply the inequality on $F$. Since $F$ has nonempty interior, an additional invertible linear choice of transverse coordinates makes an interior point of $F$ equal to $(y_1,y')=(1,0)$, while retaining the global support $y_1\ge0$.

We now construct a model with $k+1$ directions. Put $d=m-1$. For $0<\epsilon<1/2$, let $$F_\epsilon=
 \{y'\in\mathbb R^d:(\mathbf1,\epsilon,y')\in C\}.$$ This compact slice contains zero in its interior when $d>0$. Indeed, if $\overline B_r((1,0))\subset F$, contracting by $\epsilon$ about the ambient origin and then adding $(1-\epsilon)\mathbf1$ in the $s$ coordinates shows that $$\overline B_{\epsilon r}\subset F_\epsilon\subset\mathbb R^d.$$ When $d>0$, apply Lemma <a href="#lem:linear-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:linear-normalization">6</a> to choose an invertible linear map $A_\epsilon$ on $\mathbb R^d$ so that $$\overline B_1(c_\epsilon)\subset A_\epsilon F_\epsilon
 \subset\overline B_{2d},\qquad |c_\epsilon|\le d.$$ If $d=0$, take $A_\epsilon$ to be the unique linear automorphism of $\mathbb R^0$ and omit all statements about its balls. Use the affine images $$C_\epsilon=
 \{(s,r,z):(s,\epsilon r,A_\epsilon^{-1}z)\in C\}.$$ They are supported in $s_i,r\ge0$, contain the origin, and their slice at $(s,r)=(\mathbf1,1)$ has the stated normalization.

Figure <a href="#fig:thin-slice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:thin-slice">1</a> illustrates the supporting fiber and the slice used in this rescaling. The remaining step is to turn control of that one slice into a uniform bound for every fixed cap.

<figure id="fig:thin-slice">

<figcaption>The change from $(y_{1},y′)$ to $(r,z)$ at the fixed base point $s = 1$, shown schematically with one $y′$ coordinate. The actual fiber need not have the smooth or symmetric shape drawn here. The slice $y_{1} = ϵ$ becomes the slice $r = 1$, while $A_{ϵ}$ normalizes its remaining transverse coordinates. The interior point $(1,0)$ of $F$ is sent to $(ϵ^{−1},0)$. The right panel shows only this point, the origin, and the distinguished slice; the shape of the transformed fiber is omitted.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-thin-slice"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:thin-slice`)
