Visual witness description of PDF page 7 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Two-panel schematic diagram showing coordinate rescaling:
- Left panel ("Original fiber", "$s = 1$"): Cartesian axes labeled $y_1$ (vertical, pointing up) and $y'$ (horizontal, pointing right) with origin labeled $0$. An oval/egg-shaped domain $F$ contains the origin and an interior point on the vertical axis marked with a dot and labeled $(1, 0)$. A horizontal dashed line at height $\epsilon$ on the $y_1$-axis marks the slice labeled $F_\epsilon$.
- Center: Rescaling equations $r = y_1/\epsilon$ and $z = A_\epsilon y'$.
- Right panel ("Rescaled slice", "$s = 1$"): Cartesian axes labeled $r$ (vertical, pointing up) and $z$ (horizontal, pointing right) with origin labeled $0$. A tick at $1$ on the $r$-axis contains a horizontal line segment representing the slice labeled $A_\epsilon F_\epsilon$. Higher on the $r$-axis is a point marked with a dot and labeled $(\epsilon^{-1}, 0)$.
```


<a id="eq:thin-slice-cap-bound"></a>
Here is a uniform bound for their caps. Suppose $(s,r,z)\in C_\epsilon$ with $s_i,r\le T$, where $T\ge1$. Increase $s$ to $T\mathbf1$ and contract by $1/T$. This gives $$(\mathbf1,q,z/T)\in C_\epsilon,
 \qquad q=r/T\in[0,1].$$ The point $(\mathbf1,2,0)$ also belongs to $C_\epsilon$, since $2\epsilon<1$ and it is obtained by contracting $(\mathbf1,1,0)\in C$ and then increasing $s$. Interpolate these two points with weight $$a=\frac1{2-q}\in[1/2,1]$$ on $(\mathbf1,q,z/T)$. The resulting point is $(\mathbf1,1,az/T)$, so the normalized slice gives $$\label{eq:thin-slice-cap-bound}
 |z|\le 4dT\qquad(d>0).$$ Equivalently, a slice outer radius $R$ gives the bound $2TR$. When $d=0$, the bounds on $s$ and $r$ themselves suffice. Thus caps in the new nonnegative coordinates are uniformly bounded.

Extract a local limit $C_0$ as $\epsilon\downarrow0$, also taking $c_\epsilon\to c_0$ if $d>0$. For each fixed $s_i>0$ and $r\ge0$, the point $(s,r,0)$ belongs to $C_\epsilon$ as soon as $$\epsilon r\le\min\{1,s_1,\ldots,s_k\}.$$ For $r>0$, this follows by contracting $(\mathbf1,1,0)\in C$ by $\epsilon r$ and then increasing $s$; for $r=0$ it follows from the original orthant. Hence $C_0$ contains these points. Closedness adds all faces with some $s_i=0$, so it contains the entire nonnegative $(s,r)$ orthant at $z=0$. The support inequalities pass to the limit, and <a href="#eq:thin-slice-cap-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thin-slice-cap-bound">[eq:thin-slice-cap-bound]</a> makes its caps compact.

For $d>0$, the limit also contains $\{(\mathbf1,1)\}\times\overline B_1(c_0)$. The recession argument in Lemma <a href="#lem:model-fibers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:model-fibers">5</a>, which does not require full dimension, permits addition of every nonnegative $(s,r)$ direction. Thus $$[1,2]^{k+1}\times\overline B_1(c_0)\subset C_0,$$ which proves full dimension. For $d=0$, the contained orthant already has full dimension. Finally, affine invariance and diagonal closure of $\mathcal F(D)$ give $C_0\in\mathcal F(D)$. It is a $(k+1)$-model, contradicting maximality.

This contradiction allowed the initial models, fibers, and admissible coordinates to vary arbitrarily. It therefore proves the uniform assertion <a href="#eq:model-centering" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:model-centering">[eq:model-centering]</a>. Its last conclusion follows, for example, from <a href="#eq:centered-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:centered-normalization">[eq:centered-normalization]</a>. ◻

</details>

We record the quantitative geometric consequence needed for the later estimates on fixed boxes.

<a id="stmt-cor-normalized-fibers"></a>
<div id="cor:normalized-fibers" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:normalized-fiber-box"></a>
**Corollary 8**. *Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:centering" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:centering">7</a>, any chosen positive fiber of any $k$-model can be moved to $s=\mathbf1$ by positive diagonal changes in $s$ and normalized by an invertible linear change in $y$ so that $$\overline B_{r_0}\subset K_{\mathbf1}
 \subset\overline B_{R_0},
 \qquad r_0=\frac2{C_*+1},\quad R_0=2m.$$ For every fixed $0<a<1<b$, these normalized models satisfy $$\label{eq:normalized-fiber-box}
 \overline B_{ar_0}\subset K_s
 \subset\overline B_{bR_0}
 \qquad(s\in[a,b]^k).$$ Their caps for fixed positive linear forms in $s$ and fixed positive top levels have uniform bounds, and they contain a fixed interior ball about $(\mathbf1,0)$.*

*For any sequence of affine images of $D$ converging to one of these models, comparable cap and fiber bounds hold eventually, uniformly on each fixed positive box. The constants are independent of the chosen fiber; the required index may depend on its normalization. For finitely many chosen fibers, one index suffices for all of them.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-normalized-fibers"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-normalized-fibers</summary>

*Proof.* The normalization follows from Proposition <a href="#prop:centering" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:centering">7</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:linear-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:linear-normalization">6</a>. Monotonicity and contraction toward zero give $aK_{\mathbf1}\subset K_s\subset bK_{\mathbf1}$ on $[a,b]^k$, proving <a href="#eq:normalized-fiber-box" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-fiber-box">[eq:normalized-fiber-box]</a>. If $\ell(s)=\sum_i a_i s_i$ with $a_i>0$ and $B>0$, put $T=\max\{1,B/\min_i a_i\}$. Then $$C\cap\{\ell\le B\}
 \subset [0,T]^k\times\overline B_{TR_0}.$$ Also $K_s\supset\overline B_{r_0/2}$ whenever each $s_i\ge1/2$, which supplies the fixed interior ball about $(\mathbf1,0)$.

Lemma <a href="#lem:cap-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cap-compactness">3</a> transfers each cap bound to the approximants; the strict point below its top is the origin. For a fixed box $[a,b]^k$, a smaller product $[a,b]^k\times\overline B_{ar_0/2}$ is a compact subset of the interior of the model, as is seen by first enlarging the positive $s$ box slightly. Interior convergence therefore gives the lower fiber bound on the entire box, and a containing cap gives the upper bound. These constants depend only on the displayed fixed parameters. Taking the maximum of the finitely many necessary indices proves the final statement. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:area"></a>
### Affine area and a cap identity

<a id="eq:affine-area"></a>
<a id="eq:area-covariance"></a>
From this section onward, the original graph also satisfies Equation <a href="#eq:affine-maximal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-maximal">[eq:affine-maximal]</a>. We derive stationarity in arbitrary affine coordinates and a cap identity that will control the inverse-Hessian weight in the logarithmic cell estimate. The affine-area variational framework is due to Calabi (Calabi 1982) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Calabi1982](#ref-Calabi1982)]; the formulas needed here are derived explicitly. Throughout, $$\delta=\frac1{n+2}.$$ For a smooth locally strictly convex hypersurface parametrized by $X$, an *inward conormal* is a covector $\nu$ annihilating its tangent space and pointing into the convex body. Its second form is $H_{ij}=\nu X_{ij}$; we choose the sign so that $H$ is positive definite. If $\nu\xi=1$, its affine area density is $$\label{eq:affine-area}
 \mathop{}\!\mathrm dA=(\det H)^\delta
 \bigl|\det(X_1,\ldots,X_n,\xi)\bigr|^{n\delta}
 \,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dx_1\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm dx_n.$$ Changing $\xi$ by a tangent vector leaves the determinant unchanged. Multiplying $\nu$ by a positive scalar multiplies the two factors by reciprocal powers. A parameter change contributes the Jacobian to the power $2\delta+n\delta=1$, so <a href="#eq:affine-area" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-area">[eq:affine-area]</a> is a well-defined density. For the graph $X(x)=(x,u(x))$, choose $\nu=(-Du,1)$, $\xi=(0,1)$; then $$\mathop{}\!\mathrm dA=(\det D^2u)^\delta\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dx.$$ An invertible affine map with linear part $L$ replaces the conormal by $\nu L^{-1}$. The second form is unchanged and the tangential determinant acquires a factor $\det L$. Thus $$\label{eq:area-covariance}
 \mathop{}\!\mathrm dA_{L(M)}=|\det L|^{n\delta}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dA_M.$$ With the unit inward normal, the same formula reads $\mathop{}\!\mathrm dA=K^\delta\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dS$, where $K$ is Gauss curvature and $\mathop{}\!\mathrm dS$ is Euclidean hypersurface area.

<a id="stmt-lem-stationarity"></a>
<div id="lem:stationarity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Stationarity). *The equation in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> implies stationarity of affine area under every smooth compactly supported variation of the graph. Every invertible affine image of the graph is stationary under such variations as well.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-stationarity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-stationarity</summary>

*Proof.* The Hessian cofactor matrix satisfies $\partial_iU^{ij}=\partial_jU^{ij}=0$. This follows by differentiating its alternating determinant formula: terms cancel in pairs by symmetry of the third derivatives. Since $$(\det D^2u)^\delta(D^2u)^{-1}=wU,$$ the first variation in a compactly supported graph direction $\eta$ is $$\delta\int_\Omega
 \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl((D^2u)^{-1}D^2\eta\bigr)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dA
 =\delta\int_\Omega \eta U^{ij}w_{ij}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dx=0.$$ Here and below repeated coordinate indices are summed. For a compactly supported parametrized variation, its normal part can be represented locally as a graph variation, while its tangential part is a reparametrization. A partition of unity therefore gives stationarity for parametrized variations. Formula <a href="#eq:area-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:area-covariance">[eq:area-covariance]</a> transfers this statement to every affine image. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-area-bound"></a>
<div id="lem:area-bound" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Area in a ball). *For each $n$ and $R>0$, the affine area of the part in $B_R$ of any smooth positively curved convex boundary in $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ is bounded by a constant depending only on $n,R$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-area-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-area-bound</summary>

*Proof.* The Gauss map is injective: two distinct contact points with the same supporting normal would force a boundary segment in their supporting hyperplane, contrary to positive curvature. Thus $\int K\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dS$ over the part in the ball is at most the area of $S^n$.

For the Euclidean area bound, cover this part by the $2(n+1)$ sets on which some signed component of the unit normal is at least $(n+1)^{-1/2}$. On each set, convexity makes projection to the corresponding coordinate hyperplane injective: points with a given projection lie on a line meeting the body in an interval, and the chosen sign selects one endpoint. The projection Jacobian is at least $(n+1)^{-1/2}$, and its image lies in the radius-$R$ ball. This bounds $\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm dS$. Finally, Hölder’s inequality gives $$\int K^\delta\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dS
 \le \left(\int K\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dS\right)^\delta
      \left(\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm dS\right)^{1-\delta}.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-cap-identity"></a>
<div id="lem:cap-identity" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cap-identity"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Cap identity). *Let $M$ be an affine image of the original graph, bounding its convex body $D'$. Let $o\in\operatorname{int}D'$, let $\ell$ be an affine function on the ambient space, and let $\psi\in C^\infty(\mathbb R)$. Suppose that the restrictions of $\psi(\ell),\psi'(\ell),\psi''(\ell)$ vanish outside a compact subset of $M$. Then $$\label{eq:cap-identity}
 \int_M\left[
  \nu(o-X)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\ell|_{H^{-1}}^2\psi''(\ell)
  +n\bigl(\ell(o)-\ell(X)\bigr)\psi'(\ell)
  -n(n+1)\psi(\ell)
 \right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm dA=0.$$ Here $\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\ell$ is restricted to the tangent space. The first term does not depend on the scaling of the inward conormal.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cap-identity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cap-identity</summary>

*Proof.* The identity is affine invariant up to the common area factor in <a href="#eq:area-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:area-covariance">[eq:area-covariance]</a>, so it suffices to work on the original graph. Put $H=D^2u$, write $o=(o',o_{n+1})$, and use $\nu=(-Du,1)$. Set $$q(x)=\ell(x,u(x)),\qquad
 Z(x)=\nu(o-X)=o_{n+1}-u+(x-o')\cdot Du.$$ If $\ell_z$ denotes the ambient vertical coefficient of $\ell$, then $$DZ=H(x-o'),\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(H^{-1}D^2Z)=n+(x-o')\cdot D\log\det H,\qquad
 D^2q=\ell_z H.$$ Testing Lemma <a href="#lem:stationarity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stationarity">9</a> with $Z\psi(q)$ gives $$\begin{aligned}
0=\int_M\bigl[
 &Z|\mathop{}\!\mathrm dq|_{H^{-1}}^2\psi''(q)
 +nZ\ell_z\psi'(q)
 +2(x-o')\cdot Dq\,\psi'(q)\\
 &+n\psi(q)
 +\psi(q)(x-o')\cdot D\log\det H
\bigr]\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dA .
\end{aligned}$$ Because $\mathop{}\!\mathrm dA=(\det H)^\delta\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dx$, integration by parts in the last term replaces it by $$-\delta^{-1}
 \left[n\psi(q)+(x-o')\cdot Dq\,\psi'(q)\right].$$ The coefficient of $\psi$ is consequently $-n(n+1)$. The coefficient of $\psi'$ is $$n\bigl[Z\ell_z-(x-o')\cdot Dq\bigr]
 =n\bigl[\ell(o)-q\bigr].$$ This is <a href="#eq:cap-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cap-identity">[eq:cap-identity]</a>. Compact support justifies both integrations by parts. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-cap-tests"></a>
<div id="rem:cap-tests" class="remark">

*Remark 12*. For later use, $\psi$ may be convex and zero above a fixed cap height, without having compact support as a function on $\mathbb R$. If that cap is compact, its restriction to $M$ satisfies the support condition in Lemma <a href="#lem:cap-identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cap-identity">11</a>. Also, if $B_r(o)\subset D'$, the supporting-plane inequality gives <a id="eq:interior-support"></a>$$\label{eq:interior-support}
 \nu(o-X)\ge r|\nu|\qquad(X\in M).$$

</div>

<a id="sec:tubes"></a>
### An integral inequality for convex tubes

Let $E\subset\mathbb R^{n+1}$ be a closed full-dimensional convex set whose boundary is smooth, has positive second fundamental form, and is stationary for affine area. In the applications, $E$ will be an affine image of the original epigraph. Fix a splitting $$\mathbb R^{n+1}=\mathbb R^k_s\times\mathbb R^m_y,
 \qquad 2\le k\le n,\qquad m=n+1-k,$$ and an open box $\mathcal B\subset(0,\infty)^k$. Suppose that for every $s\in\mathcal B$ the fiber $$K_s=\{y\in\mathbb R^m:(s,y)\in E\}$$ is compact and contains $0$ in its interior. Our goal is the logarithmic inequality in Proposition <a href="#prop:log-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:log-inequality">13</a>, whose constant depends only on $n$, independently of the box and the shapes of its fibers.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Support coordinates and invariant measures

Fibers over compact subsets of $\mathcal B$ are uniformly bounded. Otherwise there would be $(s_j,y_j)\in E$ with $s_j$ bounded and $|y_j|\to\infty$. After passage to a subsequence, $y_j/|y_j|\to e\in S^{m-1}$. Fix $s_0\in\mathcal B$, so that $(s_0,0)\in E$. For every fixed $t\ge0$, convexity and closedness give $$(s_0,te)=\lim_{j\to\infty}
 \left[\left(1-\frac{t}{|y_j|}\right)(s_0,0)
       +\frac{t}{|y_j|}(s_j,y_j)\right]\in E.$$ This contradicts compactness of $K_{s_0}$. Thus the part of $E$ over any compact base subset is compact.

<a id="eq:tube-parametrization"></a>
The projection of $\partial E$ to the $s$ variables has full rank above $\mathcal B$. Indeed, otherwise a conormal at such a point would have zero $y$ component. Its supporting hyperplane would then give a supporting hyperplane to the projection of $E$ at an interior point of that projection, which is impossible. Thus $\partial K_s$ is smooth, and the restriction of the second fundamental form makes it strictly positively curved. Its outward Gauss map is a diffeomorphism onto $S^{m-1}$ when $m>1$. The Gauss maps also depend smoothly on $s$, since their angular differentials are invertible. Consequently the support function $h(s,e)=\max_{y\in K_s}e\cdot y$ is smooth and positive on $$\mathcal T=\mathcal B\times S^{m-1},$$ and the corresponding part of $\partial E$ is parametrized by $$\label{eq:tube-parametrization}
 X(s,e)=(s,Y(s,e)),
 \qquad Y=he+\nabla_S h.$$ Here $\nabla_S$ is the round connection. This support-function reconstruction and its angular matrix are classical; see, for example, (Trudinger and Wang 2005, preprint version, Section 7, Equations (7.15)–(7.17)) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[TW2005](#ref-TW2005)]. We compute below how they enter the second form and affine-area density of the present tube. When $m=1$, we regard $S^0=\{-1,1\}$ as the two outward directions of the interval $K_s$; Equation <a href="#eq:tube-parametrization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-parametrization">[eq:tube-parametrization]</a> then means $Y=he$ on the two endpoint sheets. Throughout this section, angular matrices in this case have size zero, their determinants equal $1$, and angular derivatives, traces, and contractions equal $0$. The measure $\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega$ on $S^0$ is counting measure.

<a id="eq:tube-area"></a>
Set $$B=-D_s^2h,
 \qquad R=\nabla_S^2h+h\mathrm{Id},
 \qquad \nu=(D_sh,-e).$$ The identities $e\cdot Y=h$, $e\cdot Y_s=h_s$, and $e\cdot\mathop{}\!\mathrm d_eY=0$ show that $\nu$ annihilates the tangent vectors of $X$. It is inward because the fiber lies on the side $e\cdot y\le h(s,e)$. Differentiating the same identities gives $$\nu X_{s_i s_j}=-h_{s_i s_j},
 \qquad \nu X_{s_i e_a}=0,
 \qquad \nu X_{e_a e_b}=R_{ab}.$$ For the last equality we used $\mathop{}\!\mathrm d_eY=R$ in round orthonormal frames. Thus the second form for this conormal is <a id="eq:tube-second-form"></a>$$\label{eq:tube-second-form}
 H=\operatorname{diag}(B,R).$$ Both blocks are positive definite. Taking $\xi=(0,-e)$ gives $\nu\xi=1$ and $|\det(X_1,\ldots,X_n,\xi)|=\det R$. The affine-area formula therefore becomes $$\label{eq:tube-area}
 \mathop{}\!\mathrm dA=I_0\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega,
 \qquad I_0=(\det B)^\delta(\det R)^{1-\delta},$$ where we used $(n+1)\delta=1-\delta$.

<a id="eq:tube-volume"></a>
To compare fibers after linear normalizations, we also need a measure invariant under separate linear changes of $s$ and $y$. The vector $V=(0,-Y)$ points from the fiber boundary toward its origin, and $\nu V=h>0$. Normalizing the conormal to take value $1$ on $V$ therefore defines the positive metric $$G=H/h.$$ Its Riemannian volume is $$\label{eq:tube-volume}
 \mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu=\mu_0\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega,
 \qquad
 \mu_0=h^{-n/2}\sqrt{\det B\,\det R}.$$ This definition makes $G$ and $\mu$ invariant under separate invertible linear changes of $s$ and $y$. To check the normalization explicitly, let $(s',y')=(As,Ly)$ and, for an old unit normal $e$, put $$t=|L^{-\mathsf T}e|,
 \qquad e'=L^{-\mathsf T}e/t.$$ The new support function obeys $h'(As,e')=h(s,e)/t$, and its conormal is $\operatorname{diag}(A,L)^{-\mathsf T}\nu/t$. Under the induced parameter map the new second form pulls back to $H/t$. Dividing it by $h'=h/t$ recovers $G$, including the change in angular parameters.

For $s$ covectors we henceforth use the inner product and norm given by $hB^{-1}$, and for angular covectors those given by $hR^{-1}$. We write both as $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle$ and $|\cdot|$, with the variables specifying the block. Thus, for example, $$|D_sg|^2=h\,(D_sg)^{\mathsf T}B^{-1}D_sg.$$ All unqualified integrals below are over $\mathcal T$. Every cutoff in the $s$ variables has compact support in $\mathcal B$, so these integrals and the corresponding variations have compact support on the hypersurface.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The stationarity equation in support coordinates

The support coordinates have identified both affine area and the invariant volume. We next express stationarity through their density ratio. This supplies the differential identity whose weighted integral will have a strictly positive quadratic part in dimensions three through nine.

We first record the cofactor identities needed for integration by parts. Since $B=D_s^2(-h)$, its cofactor is divergence free in the flat variables. The angular tensor $R$ is Codazzi: $$\nabla_aR_{bc}=\nabla_bR_{ac}.$$ One way to see this is to differentiate $\mathop{}\!\mathrm d_eY=R$ in the ambient Euclidean space. Commuting the two derivatives of $Y$ and taking tangential components gives the displayed identity; the normal components cancel because $R$ is symmetric. Equivalently, the curvature terms from commuting third derivatives of $h$ cancel the derivatives of $h\mathrm{Id}$.

<a id="eq:tube-cofactor-divergence"></a>
For completeness, if $A$ is any symmetric Codazzi tensor of positive size $r$, its cofactor is divergence free. In normal orthonormal frames the cofactor is $$(\mathop{\mathrm{cof}}A)^{ij}
 =\frac{1}{(r-1)!}
   \delta^{i i_2\cdots i_r}_{j j_2\cdots j_r}
   A_{i_2j_2}\cdots A_{i_rj_r},$$ where the symbol on the right is the alternating Kronecker symbol. On differentiating and contracting the derivative index with $i$, each resulting term contains $\nabla_iA_{i_a j_a}=\nabla_{i_a}A_{i j_a}$, symmetric in two indices against which that symbol is alternating. Each term is therefore zero. Symmetry gives the divergence identity in either contracted index. For $r=1$ the cofactor is the constant $1$; for the empty angular block no identity is needed. In particular, $$\label{eq:tube-cofactor-divergence}
 \partial_{s_i}(\mathop{\mathrm{cof}}B)^{ij}=0,
 \qquad \nabla_a(\mathop{\mathrm{cof}}R)^{ab}=0.$$

<a id="eq:tube-euler"></a>
Define <a id="eq:tube-af-definitions"></a>$$\label{eq:tube-af-definitions}
 a=\left(\frac{\det B}{\det R}\right)^\delta,
 \qquad f=\log(a/h),
 \qquad c=\frac{n+2}{2}.$$ The function $f$ records the ratio of affine area to invariant volume: by Equations <a href="#eq:tube-area" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-area">[eq:tube-area]</a> and <a href="#eq:tube-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-volume">[eq:tube-volume]</a>, $$\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm dA}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu}
 =h^{n/2}\left(\frac{\det B}{\det R}\right)^{-n/(2(n+2))}
 =\left(\frac ah\right)^{-n/2}=e^{-nf/2}.$$ A variation $h+\varepsilon\eta$, with $\eta\in C_c^\infty(\mathcal T)$, gives a compactly supported smooth variation of $X$. Positivity persists for sufficiently small $\varepsilon$ on its compact support. Differentiating Equation <a href="#eq:tube-area" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-area">[eq:tube-area]</a>, using stationarity, and dividing by $\delta$ gives $$0=\int\left[
 -I_0 B^{-1}:D_s^2\eta
 +(n+1)I_0 R^{-1}:(\nabla_S^2\eta+\eta\mathrm{Id})
 \right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega.$$ The colon denotes contraction in the indicated orthonormal frames. The scalar factors in this expression satisfy $$I_0B^{-1}=(\mathop{\mathrm{cof}}B)a^{-(n+1)},
 \qquad I_0R^{-1}=(\mathop{\mathrm{cof}}R)a.$$ Two integrations by parts using Equation <a href="#eq:tube-cofactor-divergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-cofactor-divergence">[eq:tube-cofactor-divergence]</a> therefore yield $$\label{eq:tube-euler}
 -(\mathop{\mathrm{cof}}B):D_s^2(a^{-(n+1)})
 +(n+1)(\mathop{\mathrm{cof}}R):(\nabla_S^2a+a\mathrm{Id})=0.$$ There is no angular boundary term. In the case $m=1$, the calculation is performed separately on the two endpoint sheets and the second term is absent.

<a id="eq:tube-log-euler"></a>
<a id="eq:tube-log-h"></a>
<a id="eq:tube-f-equation"></a>
Writing $t=\log a$, we have $$\begin{split}
 D_s^2(a^{-(n+1)})
   &=(n+1)a^{-(n+1)}
     \bigl(-D_s^2t+(n+1)D_st\otimes D_st\bigr),\\
 \nabla_S^2a&=a\bigl(\nabla_S^2t+
                        \nabla_St\otimes\nabla_St\bigr).
 \end{split}$$ Dividing Equation <a href="#eq:tube-euler" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-euler">[eq:tube-euler]</a> by $(n+1)I_0$ gives $$\label{eq:tube-log-euler}
 \begin{split}
 B^{-1}:D_s^2\log a
 &-(n+1)(D_s\log a)^{\mathsf T}B^{-1}D_s\log a\\
 {}+R^{-1}:\nabla_S^2\log a
 &+(\nabla_S\log a)^{\mathsf T}R^{-1}\nabla_S\log a
 +\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}R^{-1}=0.
 \end{split}$$ Introduce $$\begin{gathered}
 p=D_s\log h,\qquad d=D_sf,
 \qquad p_e=\nabla_S\log h,\qquad d_e=\nabla_Sf,\\
 T_sg=hB^{-1}:D_s^2g,
 \qquad T_eg=hR^{-1}:\nabla_S^2g.
 \end{gathered}$$ Direct differentiation of $h$ gives $$\label{eq:tube-log-h}
 T_s\log h=-k-|p|^2,
 \qquad
 T_e\log h=n-k-h\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}R^{-1}-|p_e|^2.$$ Substitute $\log a=\log h+f$ in Equation <a href="#eq:tube-log-euler" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-log-euler">[eq:tube-log-euler]</a> and multiply by $h$. The angular trace and $|p_e|^2$ terms cancel, and Equation <a href="#eq:tube-log-h" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-log-h">[eq:tube-log-h]</a> gives $$\label{eq:tube-f-equation}
 n-2k+T_sf-|p|^2-(n+1)|p+d|^2
 +T_ef+2\langle p_e,d_e\rangle+|d_e|^2=0.$$

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Weighted integration and coercivity

Equation <a href="#eq:tube-f-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-f-equation">[eq:tube-f-equation]</a> contains derivatives with different drifts in the base and angular variables. We integrate with the invariant measure $\mu$, retaining both drifts until the cancellation in Equation <a href="#eq:tube-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-cancellation">[eq:tube-cancellation]</a>. The dimension restriction enters only in the quadratic form that remains.

<a id="eq:tube-matrix-densities"></a>
The matrix densities for $T_s$ and $T_e$ relative to $\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega$ are $$\label{eq:tube-matrix-densities}
 \mu_0hB^{-1}=(\mathop{\mathrm{cof}}B)h^{-n}e^{-cf},
 \qquad
 \mu_0hR^{-1}=(\mathop{\mathrm{cof}}R)h^2e^{cf}.$$ For example, the first follows from $\sqrt{\det R/\det B}=a^{-c}$ and $h^{1-n/2}a^{-c}=h^{-n}e^{-cf}$; the second follows in the same way with the signs of the exponents reversed.

<a id="eq:tube-integration-parts"></a>
<a id="eq:tube-p-identity"></a>
<a id="eq:tube-cancellation"></a>
Let $v\in C_c^\infty(\mathcal B)$. The logarithmic derivatives of the scalar factors in Equation <a href="#eq:tube-matrix-densities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-matrix-densities">[eq:tube-matrix-densities]</a> are $-np-cd$ and $2p_e+cd_e$, respectively. One integration by parts and Equation <a href="#eq:tube-cofactor-divergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-cofactor-divergence">[eq:tube-cofactor-divergence]</a> therefore give, for every smooth $g$ on $\mathcal T$, $$\label{eq:tube-integration-parts}
 \begin{split}
 \int v^2T_sg\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu
 &=\int\left[
   v^2\langle np+cd,D_sg\rangle
   -2v\langle D_sv,D_sg\rangle\right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu,\\
 \int v^2T_eg\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu
 &=-\int v^2\langle2p_e+cd_e,\nabla_Sg\rangle\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu.
 \end{split}$$ The angular identity has no cutoff term because $v$ depends only on $s$. Taking $g=\log h$ in the first identity and using Equation <a href="#eq:tube-log-h" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-log-h">[eq:tube-log-h]</a> yields $$\label{eq:tube-p-identity}
 \int v^2\left[k+(n+1)|p|^2+c\langle d,p\rangle\right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu
 =2\int v\langle D_sv,p\rangle\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu.$$ Next integrate Equation <a href="#eq:tube-f-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-f-equation">[eq:tube-f-equation]</a> against $v^2$ and apply Equation <a href="#eq:tube-integration-parts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-integration-parts">[eq:tube-integration-parts]</a> with $g=f$. Expanding the squares gives <a id="eq:tube-d-identity"></a>$$\label{eq:tube-d-identity}
 \begin{split}
 \int v^2\biggl[\frac n2\bigl(|d|^2+|d_e|^2\bigr)
  +(n+2)\bigl(|p|^2+\langle p,d\rangle\bigr)
  -(n-2k)\biggr]\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu\\
 =-2\int v\langle D_sv,d\rangle\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu.
 \end{split}$$ In particular, subtracting twice Equation <a href="#eq:tube-p-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-p-identity">[eq:tube-p-identity]</a> gives the exact cancellation $$\label{eq:tube-cancellation}
 \begin{split}
 \frac n2\int v^2
  \bigl(|d|^2+|d_e|^2-2|p|^2-2\bigr)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu
 ={}&-2\int v\langle D_sv,d\rangle\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu\\
    &-4\int v\langle D_sv,p\rangle\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu.
 \end{split}$$

<a id="eq:tube-coercive-identity"></a>
<a id="eq:tube-energy"></a>
We combine these identities to control both the $\mu$-mass and the derivative terms. For a parameter $\lambda>0$, add $2\lambda/n$ times Equation <a href="#eq:tube-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-cancellation">[eq:tube-cancellation]</a> to Equation <a href="#eq:tube-p-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-p-identity">[eq:tube-p-identity]</a>. The resulting left integrand is $$F_\lambda=k-2\lambda
 +(n+1-2\lambda)|p|^2+c\langle p,d\rangle
 +\lambda\bigl(|d|^2+|d_e|^2\bigr),$$ and the identity is $$\label{eq:tube-coercive-identity}
 \int v^2F_\lambda\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu
 =\int v\left\langle D_sv,
       \left(2-\frac{8\lambda}{n}\right)p
                -\frac{4\lambda}{n}d\right\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu.$$ In an orthonormal frame for the $s$ covectors, the coefficient matrix for each paired component of $p,d$ is $$\begin{pmatrix}
 n+1-2\lambda&(n+2)/4\\
 (n+2)/4&\lambda
 \end{pmatrix}.$$ At $\lambda=1$, its determinant is $$n-1-\frac{(n+2)^2}{16}=\frac{(n-2)(10-n)}{16}.$$ This is positive for $3\le n\le9$, but the scalar term $k-2$ vanishes when $k=2$. Choosing $\lambda$ slightly below one makes the scalar term positive while preserving the positive quadratic form. For the rest of the argument impose $3\le n\le9$ and fix $\lambda=15/16$, which works throughout this range. Then $k-2\lambda\ge1/8$, and the determinant satisfies $$\lambda(n+1-2\lambda)-\frac{(n+2)^2}{16}
 =\frac{-8n^2+88n-137}{128}\ge\frac7{128}
 \qquad(3\le n\le9),$$ where the last inequality follows by concavity of the quadratic and its endpoint values. Both diagonal entries are positive. Thus there is a constant $\kappa_n>0$ such that $$F_\lambda\ge
 \kappa_n\bigl(1+|p|^2+|d|^2+|d_e|^2\bigr).$$ Young’s inequality absorbs the right side of Equation <a href="#eq:tube-coercive-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-coercive-identity">[eq:tube-coercive-identity]</a>, giving $$\label{eq:tube-energy}
 \int v^2\bigl(1+|p|^2+|d|^2+|d_e|^2\bigr)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu
 \le C_n\int|D_sv|^2\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu.$$ All constants here depend only on $n$; the estimates are uniform for $2\le k\le n$.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The logarithmic inequality

<a id="eq:log-measure"></a>
For $1\le i\le k$, put $\alpha_i=\log s_i$ and define the measure $$\label{eq:log-measure}
 \mathop{}\!\mathrm d\sigma=\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^k|D_s\log s_i|^2\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu,
 \qquad |D_s\log s_i|^2=\frac{h(B^{-1})_{ii}}{s_i^2}.$$ This measure is invariant under invertible linear changes in $y$ and positive diagonal changes in $s$: the metric $G$ and its volume are invariant, while each $\alpha_i$ changes only by an additive constant.

<a id="stmt-prop-log-inequality"></a>
<div id="prop:log-inequality" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:log-inequality"></a>
**Proposition 13**. *Let $3\le n\le9$ and $2\le k\le n$. On any tube satisfying the hypotheses of this section, there is a constant $C_n$, depending only on $n$, such that every $\widetilde v\in C_c^\infty(\log\mathcal B)$ satisfies $$\label{eq:log-inequality}
 \int_{\mathcal T}\widetilde v(\log s)^2\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\sigma
 \le C_n\int_{\mathcal T}
       |\nabla\widetilde v(\log s)|_{\mathbb R^k}^2\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\sigma.$$ Here $\log s=(\log s_1,\ldots,\log s_k)$ and the gradient on the right is Euclidean. No completeness of the metric $G$ is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-log-inequality"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-log-inequality</summary>

*Proof.* Since $T_s\alpha_i=-|D_s\alpha_i|^2$, Equation <a href="#eq:tube-integration-parts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-integration-parts">[eq:tube-integration-parts]</a> gives $$\begin{split}
 \int v^2|D_s\alpha_i|^2\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu
 ={}&-\int v^2\langle np+cd,D_s\alpha_i\rangle\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu\\
    &+2\int v\langle D_sv,D_s\alpha_i\rangle\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu.
 \end{split}$$ Young’s inequality, followed by Equation <a href="#eq:tube-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-energy">[eq:tube-energy]</a>, therefore gives $$\int v^2|D_s\alpha_i|^2\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu
 \le C_n\int|D_sv|^2\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu.$$ Summing these inequalities and the term controlling $\int v^2\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu$ in Equation <a href="#eq:tube-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-energy">[eq:tube-energy]</a>, we obtain $$\int v^2\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\sigma\le C_n\int|D_sv|^2\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu.$$ Finally take $v(s)=\widetilde v(\log s)$. The chain rule and Cauchy–Schwarz in the positive inner product $hB^{-1}$ imply $$\begin{split}
 |D_sv|^2
 &=\left|\sum_{i=1}^k
       (\partial_i\widetilde v)(\log s)D_s\alpha_i\right|^2\\
 &\le |\nabla\widetilde v(\log s)|_{\mathbb R^k}^2
       \sum_{i=1}^k|D_s\alpha_i|^2.
 \end{split}$$ Equation <a href="#eq:log-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:log-inequality">[eq:log-inequality]</a> follows from Equation <a href="#eq:log-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:log-measure">[eq:log-measure]</a>. Every integration used a compactly supported cutoff on the smooth tube, which also proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:bounds"></a>
### Excluding models with several directions

We now combine the logarithmic inequality with estimates on smooth approximants of a model. All curvature quantities in this section belong to the approximants; no differentiability of the limiting body is used.

<a id="stmt-prop-one-direction"></a>
<div id="prop:one-direction" class="proposition">

**Proposition 14**. *Suppose $3\le n\le9$. The largest number of directions of a model in $\mathcal F(D)$, in the sense of Definition <a href="#def:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:model">4</a>, is one.*

</div>

We first establish the area estimate that prevents the measures in the logarithmic inequality from vanishing. The comparison uses concavity of the affine-area integrand and stationarity, as in the local maximization argument of Trudinger and Wang (Trudinger and Wang 2000, sec. 6, Equations (6.2)–(6.4)) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[TW2000](#ref-TW2000)]. Here the comparison is applied to uniformly normalized caps of smooth approximants.

<a id="stmt-lem-positive-cap-area"></a>
<div id="lem:positive-cap-area" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15**. *Let $D_j$ be affine images of $D$ converging locally to a full-dimensional closed convex set $C$. Suppose that a nonzero linear function $\ell$ and a number $b$ satisfy $$C\cap\{\ell\le b\}\ \text{is compact},
 \qquad
 \operatorname{int}C\cap\{\ell<b\}\ne\varnothing.$$ There are $c>0$ and $j_0$ such that $$\int_{\partial D_j\cap\{\ell<b\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm dA_j\ge c
 \qquad(j\ge j_0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-positive-cap-area"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-positive-cap-area</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#lem:cap-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cap-compactness">3</a> gives a uniform bound for these caps. Choose a closed ball $\overline B_r(o)$ in $\operatorname{int}C\cap\{\ell<b\}$. After decreasing $r$, the same ball lies in $D_j$ for all sufficiently large $j$. Fix $\varepsilon_0>0$ with $\ell(o)\le b-\varepsilon_0$.

Let $e_j$ be the unit vector in the image of the original upward vertical direction under the affine map defining $D_j$. It is a recession direction of $D_j$, and $\partial D_j$ is graphical along it. The component $\ell(e_j)$ is bounded below by a positive constant. Indeed, the ray $o+t e_j$ lies in $D_j$; if $\ell(e_j)\le0$, the whole ray lies in the cap, and otherwise its initial segment of length $(b-\ell(o))/\ell(e_j)$ lies there. Uniform boundedness of the caps excludes both a nonpositive component and components tending to zero. Consequently $$V_j=\frac{e_j}{\ell(e_j)}
 \quad\text{satisfies}\quad
 \ell(V_j)=1,\qquad |V_j|\le C.$$ Use the coordinates $$X=\zeta+\tau V_j,\qquad \zeta\in\ker\ell.$$ These coordinate maps and their inverses are uniformly bounded. With an orthonormal basis of $\ker\ell$, their determinants have absolute value $1/|\ell|$, where $|\ell|$ is the Euclidean norm of the covector. In these coordinates $\partial D_j$ is the graph of a smooth strictly convex function $g_j$, and the cap corresponds to $\tau\le b$.

The sets $$E_j=\{\zeta:g_j(\zeta)<b\}$$ are uniformly bounded. Every closed sublevel of $g_j$ at a level at most $b$ is compact within its domain: its lifted graph lies in a compact cap, and the closed hypersurface $\partial D_j$ is precisely the graph in this direction. Writing $\zeta_j=o-\ell(o)V_j$, the common interior ball gives $$g_j(\zeta)\le b-\varepsilon_0
 \qquad\bigl(\zeta\in B_{\ker\ell}(\zeta_j,r)\bigr).$$ Here $B_{\ker\ell}(\zeta_j,r)$ is the radius-$r$ ball in $\ker\ell$ centered at $\zeta_j$. The centers $\zeta_j$ and the sets $E_j$ are uniformly bounded. We may therefore choose one $\alpha>0$ so small that the quadratics $$q_j(\zeta)=b-\frac{2\varepsilon_0}{3}
                +\alpha|\zeta-\zeta_j|^2$$ satisfy $$b-\frac{2\varepsilon_0}{3}\le q_j
       \le b-\frac{\varepsilon_0}{3}
 \qquad\text{on }E_j.$$

Let $\chi$ be a smooth convex regularization of the positive part, with $0\le\chi'\le1$, equal to $0$ for arguments at most $-\eta$ and equal to the argument for arguments at least $\eta$, where $0<\eta<\varepsilon_0/6$. On $E_j$ set $$\widetilde g_j=g_j+\chi(q_j-g_j).$$ This equals $q_j$ on the indicated base ball and equals $g_j$ near the level $b$. Extending by $g_j$ outside $E_j$ gives a smooth perturbation whose difference from $g_j$ has compact support in $E_j$. Its Hessian is positive definite, since it is a convex combination of $D^2g_j$ and $D^2q_j$, plus a positive semidefinite matrix.

The function $F(H)=(\det H)^\delta$ is concave on positive definite matrices: the determinant $n$-th root is concave, and $n\delta=n/(n+2)<1$. Concavity and Lemma <a href="#lem:stationarity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stationarity">9</a> give $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{E_j}F(D^2\widetilde g_j)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\zeta
 &\le \int_{E_j}F(D^2g_j)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\zeta\\
 &\quad+\delta\int_{E_j}
 F(D^2g_j)(D^2g_j)^{-1}:D^2(\widetilde g_j-g_j)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\zeta\\
 &=\int_{E_j}F(D^2g_j)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\zeta.
\end{aligned}$$ The first integral is bounded below by $\lvert B_{\ker\ell}(0,r)\rvert(2\alpha)^{n\delta}>0$. Affine covariance of area, with the uniformly controlled coordinate determinants above, proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Positive mass away from the coordinate faces

<a id="eq:cap-area-positive"></a>
Fix a model $C$ with the maximal number $k$ of directions, and choose affine images $D_j\to C$. Write its coordinates as $(s,y)\in\mathbb R^k\times\mathbb R^m$, where $m=n+1-k$. Choose $b>0$ so that the cap for $\ell_0(s)=s_1+\cdots+s_k$ contains an interior ball strictly below its top. Such a choice is possible because $C$ has nonempty interior. The cap is compact by Definition <a href="#def:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:model">4</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:positive-cap-area" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:positive-cap-area">15</a> gives $$\label{eq:cap-area-positive}
 A_j\bigl(\partial D_j\cap\{\ell_0<b\}\bigr)\ge c>0$$ for large $j$, and all these caps lie in a fixed ball.

<a id="eq:strip-area"></a>
For any fixed $0<\varepsilon<1$, convergence and the uniform cap bound imply that $s_i\ge-\varepsilon$ everywhere on the approximant caps once $j$ is sufficiently large. In the part where $s_i\le\varepsilon$, stretch the single coordinate $s_i$ by $1/\varepsilon$. The transformed boundary part lies in a fixed ball, independent of $\varepsilon$ and $j$. Lemma <a href="#lem:area-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:area-bound">10</a> and the $|\det L|^{n\delta}$ covariance of affine area give $$\label{eq:strip-area}
 A_j\bigl(\partial D_j\cap\{\ell_0<b,\ s_i\le\varepsilon\}\bigr)
       \le C\varepsilon^{n\delta}.$$ Only the boundary part under consideration needs to lie in the ball. Choose $\varepsilon$ so small that the sum of these $k$ bounds is less than $c/2$, and then choose $j$ large enough. Equations <a href="#eq:cap-area-positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cap-area-positive">[eq:cap-area-positive]</a> and <a href="#eq:strip-area" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strip-area">[eq:strip-area]</a> leave area at least $c/2$ in a fixed bounded region where all $s_i>\varepsilon$.

<a id="eq:transverse-dimension"></a>
If $m=0$, the model is the nonnegative orthant in $\mathbb R^{n+1}$. The remaining region is contained in a compact subset of its interior, which lies in $\operatorname{int}D_j$ for large $j$. It cannot contain any points of $\partial D_j$, a contradiction. Hence $$\label{eq:transverse-dimension}
 k\le n,\qquad m\ge1.$$

For the rest of the section, suppose that $k\ge2$. On every fixed compact box of positive $s$’s, the fibers of $D_j$ are uniformly bounded and contain zero in their interiors for large $j$. This follows from Proposition <a href="#prop:centering" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:centering">7</a>, Lemma <a href="#lem:model-fibers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:model-fibers">5</a>, and Lemma <a href="#lem:cap-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cap-compactness">3</a>. Thus the tube coordinates and measures <a href="#eq:tube-area" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-area">[eq:tube-area]</a>, <a href="#eq:tube-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-volume">[eq:tube-volume]</a>, and <a href="#eq:log-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:log-measure">[eq:log-measure]</a> are available there. For a base set $E\subset(0,\infty)^k$, write $\mu_j(E)$ and $\sigma_j(E)$ for the masses of $E\times S^{m-1}$. In particular, define $$Q_L=\{s\in(0,\infty)^k:|\log s|_\infty\le L\},
 \qquad
 \log s=(\log s_1,\ldots,\log s_k).$$

<a id="eq:positive-log-mass"></a>
The preceding area bound gives a fixed positive box $Q$ such that $\int_{Q\times S^{m-1}}I_0\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega\ge c/2$ for large $j$. On this box $h$ is uniformly bounded above, and $$\int_{Q\times S^{m-1}}\det R\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega\le C.$$ Indeed, $\det R\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega$ is Euclidean area on the boundary of a uniformly bounded convex fiber. When $m=1$, it is counting measure on the two endpoints, with $\det R=1$. Using the density $\mu_0$ from <a href="#eq:tube-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tube-volume">[eq:tube-volume]</a>, the exact factorization $$I_0=\mu_0^{2\delta}h^{n\delta}(\det R)^{1-2\delta}$$ and Hölder’s inequality yield $$\frac c2\le\int_{Q\times S^{m-1}} I_0\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega
       \le C\mu_j(Q)^{2\delta}.$$ Since $\sigma_j\ge\mu_j$, enlarging $Q$ to a fixed $Q_{L_0}$ proves that there are $L_0,c_0>0$ with $$\label{eq:positive-log-mass}
 \sigma_j(Q_{L_0})\ge c_0
 \qquad\text{for all sufficiently large }j.$$

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### A uniform bound on each logarithmic cell

We now have positive mass in one fixed logarithmic box. To control growth, we need a bound for the mass of every cell that is independent of its logarithmic position. Centering provides this uniformity after affine normalization. The cap identity controls an inverse-Hessian integral, and bounds for Hessian minors provide the complementary factor in a weighted interpolation.

<a id="stmt-lem-log-cell-bound"></a>
<div id="lem:log-cell-bound" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16**. *There is a constant $C_{\mathrm{cell}}$, independent of $a\in\mathbb R^k$, such that $$\sigma_j\left\{s:\tfrac12e^{a_i}\le s_i\le2e^{a_i}
                       \text{ for every }i\right\}
 \le C_{\mathrm{cell}}$$ for every sufficiently large $j$. The threshold for $j$ may depend on $a$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-log-cell-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-log-cell-bound</summary>

*Proof.* Rescale $s_i$ by $e^{-a_i}$ and make an invertible linear change in $y$. By Corollary <a href="#cor:normalized-fibers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:normalized-fibers">8</a>, these changes can be chosen so that the limit fiber above $\mathbf1=(1,\ldots,1)$ satisfies $$B_{r_*}\subset K_{\mathbf1}\subset B_{R_*},$$ where $r_*,R_*>0$ are independent of $a$. We use the same affine change on the approximants. The measures $\sigma_j$ are preserved by these normalizations.

<a id="eq:normalized-fiber-bounds"></a>
Corollary <a href="#cor:normalized-fibers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:normalized-fibers">8</a> also gives uniform fiber bounds on the fixed box $[1/4,4]^k$, uniform bounds for caps with fixed positive linear forms and fixed top levels, and a fixed interior ball centered at $o=(\mathbf1,0)$. Applying its approximation statement on a slightly larger positive box gives $$\label{eq:normalized-fiber-bounds}
 0<c_1\le h\le C_1
 \quad\text{on }[1/4,4]^k\times S^{m-1}$$ and the same uniform cap and interior-ball bounds for all sufficiently large $j$. These constants are independent of $a$; only the required index depends on the normalization.

<a id="eq:cap-inverse-hessian"></a>
Let $Q=[1/2,2]^k$ and $\mathcal T=Q\times S^{m-1}$. By <a href="#eq:log-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:log-measure">[eq:log-measure]</a> and <a href="#eq:normalized-fiber-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-fiber-bounds">[eq:normalized-fiber-bounds]</a>, $$\sigma_j(Q)\le C\int_{\mathcal T}
       \sqrt{\det B\,\det R}\,(1+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B^{-1})
       \,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega,$$ since $s_i^{-2}\le4$ on $Q$. To control the inverse-Hessian factor in this integral, we first prove $$\label{eq:cap-inverse-hessian}
 \int_{\mathcal T} I_0\,\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B^{-1}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega\le C.$$ For $r=1,\ldots,k$, set $$a^{(r)}=\mathbf1+e_r,\qquad \ell_r(s)=a^{(r)}\cdot s.$$ Their values on $Q$ lie in $J=[(k+1)/2,\,2(k+1)]$. Choose a smooth nonnegative function $\rho$, compactly supported below a fixed number $b_0>2(k+1)$, with $\rho\ge1$ on $J$, and put $$\psi(t)=\int_t^{b_0}(r-t)\rho(r)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dr.$$ Then $\psi''=\rho\ge0$, and $\psi$ vanishes above $b_0$. Although it is affine sufficiently far below, its surface test is compactly supported in the bounded cap $\ell_r\le b_0$. The functions $\psi,\psi'$ and the levels $\ell_r$ are bounded there by uniform constants.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:cap-identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cap-identity">11</a> with the center $o$ of the fixed interior ball. If its radius is $r_0$, the supporting-plane inequality gives $$\nu(o-X)\ge r_0|\nu|.$$ The integrand involving $\psi''$ in that identity is nonnegative over the whole cap. On the tube, take the conormal $\nu=(D_sh,-e)$; it has norm at least one, and $$|d\ell_r|_{H^{-1}}^2=(a^{(r)})^{\mathsf T}B^{-1}a^{(r)}.$$ The other terms in the cap identity have bounded integrals by Lemma <a href="#lem:area-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:area-bound">10</a> and the uniform cap bound. Hence $$\int_{\mathcal T} I_0\,
       (a^{(r)})^{\mathsf T}B^{-1}a^{(r)}
       \,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega\le C.$$ Summing over $r$ proves <a href="#eq:cap-inverse-hessian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cap-inverse-hessian">[eq:cap-inverse-hessian]</a>, since $$\sum_{r=1}^k a^{(r)}(a^{(r)})^{\mathsf T}
       =\mathrm{Id}+(k+2)\mathbf1\mathbf1^{\mathsf T}\ \ge\ \mathrm{Id}.$$

<a id="eq:hessian-minor-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:fiber-area-bound"></a>
We next claim that $$\label{eq:hessian-minor-bound}
 \int_{\mathcal T}\det B\,(1+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B^{-1})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega\le C.$$ For each fixed $e$, the function $-h(\,\cdot\,,e)$ is strictly convex. Its gradient is uniformly bounded on $Q$ by <a href="#eq:normalized-fiber-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-fiber-bounds">[eq:normalized-fiber-bounds]</a> on the larger box. For a principal minor, hold the unused $s$-coordinates fixed and consider the gradient in the selected coordinates. It is injective, because the corresponding Hessian is positive definite, and its image lies in a fixed bounded box. The change-of-variables formula bounds the integral of its Jacobian, which is precisely that principal minor. Integrating in the unused coordinates and in $e$ gives a uniform bound. The determinant itself is the full principal minor, and $\det B\,\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B^{-1}$ is the sum of the principal minors of order $k-1$. This proves <a href="#eq:hessian-minor-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hessian-minor-bound">[eq:hessian-minor-bound]</a>. As before, the uniform fiber bound also gives $$\label{eq:fiber-area-bound}
 \int_{\mathcal T}\det R\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega\le C.$$

<a id="eq:weighted-interpolation"></a>
Equations <a href="#eq:hessian-minor-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hessian-minor-bound">[eq:hessian-minor-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:fiber-area-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fiber-area-bound">[eq:fiber-area-bound]</a>, together with Cauchy–Schwarz, give $$\int_{\mathcal T}\sqrt{\det B\,\det R}
       \,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega\le C.$$ For the inverse-Hessian weight, set $$\theta=\frac1{2(1-\delta)}=\frac{n+2}{2(n+1)},
 \qquad t_B=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B^{-1}.$$ The interpolation identity is $$\label{eq:weighted-interpolation}
 \sqrt{\det B\,\det R}\,t_B
       =(I_0t_B)^\theta(\det B\,t_B)^{1-\theta}.$$ Since $0<\theta<1$, Hölder’s inequality applied to <a href="#eq:weighted-interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-interpolation">[eq:weighted-interpolation]</a>, using <a href="#eq:cap-inverse-hessian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cap-inverse-hessian">[eq:cap-inverse-hessian]</a> and <a href="#eq:hessian-minor-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hessian-minor-bound">[eq:hessian-minor-bound]</a>, gives $$\int_{\mathcal T}\sqrt{\det B\,\det R}\,
           \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B^{-1}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\omega\le C.$$ The last two bounds control both terms in the target integral and hence $\sigma_j(Q)$. Its invariance under the preceding coordinate changes proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### The growth contradiction

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-log-cell-bound-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-log-cell-bound</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:one-direction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:one-direction">14</a>.* A model with one direction exists by Lemma <a href="#lem:proper-sections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:proper-sections">2</a>. Suppose that the maximal number $k$ is at least two, and fix the model and approximants used above. The case $k=n+1$ has already been excluded in <a href="#eq:transverse-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transverse-dimension">[eq:transverse-dimension]</a>; hence $2\le k\le n$.

<a id="eq:polynomial-log-growth"></a>
Covering a logarithmic box by finitely many of the cells in Lemma <a href="#lem:log-cell-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:log-cell-bound">16</a> gives a constant $C_{\mathrm{box}}$ such that, for every fixed $L\ge1$, $$\label{eq:polynomial-log-growth}
 \sigma_j(Q_L)\le C_{\mathrm{box}}(L+1)^k
 \qquad\text{for every sufficiently large }j.$$ The constant is independent of $L$ and $j$. The index threshold may depend on $L$, because only finitely many cells are involved for each fixed $L$. By increasing that threshold, the tube coordinates are also available on $Q_{L+2}$.

<a id="eq:exponential-log-growth"></a>
For an integer $l\ge1$, choose a smooth cutoff $\widetilde v_l$ on $\mathbb R^k$, equal to one on $\{|t|_\infty\le l\}$, zero outside $\{|t|_\infty<l+1\}$, and with a uniformly bounded Euclidean gradient. Its gradient is supported in the intervening shell. Proposition <a href="#prop:log-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:log-inequality">13</a>, applied with $v_l(s)=\widetilde v_l(\log s)$, implies $$\sigma_j(Q_l)
       \le C_{\mathrm{grow}}
            \bigl(\sigma_j(Q_{l+1})-\sigma_j(Q_l)\bigr)$$ whenever the tube is available through $Q_{l+1}$. Here $C_{\mathrm{grow}}$ is independent of $l$ and $j$. Box boundaries have zero mass, because these measures have smooth densities in the tube coordinates. Writing $q=1+C_{\mathrm{grow}}^{-1}>1$ and taking an integer $l_0\ge\max(1,L_0)$, iteration from <a href="#eq:positive-log-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-log-mass">[eq:positive-log-mass]</a> gives $$\label{eq:exponential-log-growth}
 \sigma_j(Q_L)\ge c_0 q^{L-l_0}
 \qquad(L\ge l_0,\ L\text{ an integer})$$ for approximants on which all the indicated estimates hold.

Choose one finite integer $L$ large enough that $$c_0q^{L-l_0}>C_{\mathrm{box}}(L+1)^k.$$ Then choose one $j$ large enough for the initial mass bound, the finitely many cell bounds through $Q_L$, and tube coordinates through $Q_{L+2}$. For this same $j$, <a href="#eq:polynomial-log-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polynomial-log-growth">[eq:polynomial-log-growth]</a> and <a href="#eq:exponential-log-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exponential-log-growth">[eq:exponential-log-growth]</a> contradict each other. Thus no maximal model has $k\ge2$, proving the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:rigidity"></a>
### Section balance and rigidity

We now convert the exclusion of models with two or more directions into uniform control of tangent sections. This control supplies the modulus of convexity needed for the interior estimates of Trudinger and Wang.

<a id="stmt-lem-uniform-balance"></a>
<div id="lem:uniform-balance" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:uniform-section-balance"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Uniform balance of tangent sections). *There is a constant $0<\rho\le1$ such that, for every $a\in\Omega$ and $t>0$, the compact convex body $$K_a(t)=\overline{S_u(a,t)}-a$$ satisfies $$\label{eq:uniform-section-balance}
 -\rho K_a(t)\subset K_a(t).$$ The same $\rho$ works for all base points and heights.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-uniform-balance"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-uniform-balance</summary>

*Proof.* For a fixed $a$, use the ambient affine coordinates $$s=z-l_a(x),\qquad y=x-a.$$ The epigraph lies in $s\ge0$, contains the ray $\{s\ge0,\ y=0\}$, and has compact caps by Lemma <a href="#lem:proper-sections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:proper-sections">2</a>. It is therefore a model with one direction, whose fiber at height $t$ is $K_a(t)$. Proposition <a href="#prop:one-direction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:one-direction">14</a> says that the maximal direction number is one. The uniformity over models and coordinates in Proposition <a href="#prop:centering" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:centering">7</a> consequently gives $-K_a(t)\subset C K_a(t)$ with one finite constant $C$. Taking $\rho=\min\{1,C^{-1}\}$ proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-doubling-entire"></a>
<div id="lem:doubling-entire" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:gap-doubling"></a>
<a id="eq:doubling-constants"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Doubling and extension of rays). *The domain is $\Omega=\mathbb R^n$. Moreover, there are constants $\beta>1$ and $A>1$, depending only on $\rho$, such that every tangent-subtracted line restriction $$g(r)=u(a+re)-u(a)-r\,Du(a)\cdot e$$ satisfies $$\label{eq:gap-doubling}
 g(\beta r)\le A g(r)\qquad(r>0).$$ One may take $$\label{eq:doubling-constants}
 \beta=1+\frac{\rho}{2},\qquad
 A=\frac{(2+\rho)(1+\rho)}{\rho}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-doubling-entire"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-doubling-entire</summary>

*Proof.* Initially let $I=\{r:a+re\in\Omega\}$, an open interval containing zero, and choose $R\in I$ with $R>0$. For $0<r_0<R$, the tangent section based at $a+r_0e$ and having height $$b=r_0g'(r_0)-g(r_0)>0$$ contains $a$ on its boundary. Applying <a href="#eq:uniform-section-balance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-section-balance">[eq:uniform-section-balance]</a> to the displacement $-r_0e$ puts the displacement $\rho r_0e$ in the same translated section. In particular $(1+\rho)r_0\in I$, and the section inequality gives $$g((1+\rho)r_0)\le(1+\rho)r_0g'(r_0).$$ Convexity and $g\ge0$ give $$g'(r_0)\le
 \frac{g(R)-g(r_0)}{R-r_0}
 \le\frac{g(R)}{R-r_0}.$$ Set $$\theta=\frac{2+\rho}{2(1+\rho)},\qquad r_0=\theta R.$$ Then $(1+\rho)\theta=\beta$, with $\beta$ as in <a href="#eq:doubling-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:doubling-constants">[eq:doubling-constants]</a>, and $$\beta R\in I,\qquad
 g(\beta R)\le\frac{\beta}{1-\theta}g(R)=A g(R).$$ Iterating the domain inclusion shows that the positive ray from $a$ in direction $e$ is contained in $\Omega$. Every oriented direction admits an initial positive interval by openness, so all rays from $a$ lie in $\Omega$. This proves $\Omega=\mathbb R^n$, as well as <a href="#eq:gap-doubling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gap-doubling">[eq:gap-doubling]</a> on every ray. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:normalized-sections"></a>
Fix a base point, translate it to zero, and subtract its tangent, so that $u(0)=0$, $Du(0)=0$, and $u\ge0$. Each section $S_u(0,t)$ can be normalized by a linear map that preserves zero, with constants uniform in $t$. For $K=\overline{S_u(0,t)}$, apply Lemma <a href="#lem:linear-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:linear-normalization">6</a> with $d=n$ and $C_*=\rho^{-1}$. It gives an invertible linear map $L$ with $$\overline{B_{2\rho/(1+\rho)}}\subset LK
 \subset\overline{B_{2n}}.$$ A scalar adjustment therefore produces invertible maps $A_t$ and a fixed $R\ge1$, depending only on $n,\rho$, for which $$\label{eq:normalized-sections}
 v_t(y)=t^{-1}u(A_ty),\qquad
 B_1\subset S_{v_t}(0,1)\subset B_R.$$ The scalar adjustment may be made with a strict margin, so the inner inclusion holds for the open section. Balance and <a href="#eq:gap-doubling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gap-doubling">[eq:gap-doubling]</a> are unchanged by these horizontal linear maps and positive vertical rescalings.

<a id="stmt-lem-normalized-modulus"></a>
<div id="lem:normalized-modulus" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:normalized-modulus"></a>
<a id="eq:uniform-strict-convexity"></a>
**Lemma 19** (A uniform normalized modulus). *For all functions $v_t$ in <a href="#eq:normalized-sections" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-sections">[eq:normalized-sections]</a>, there is one positive function $b:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)$ such that $$\label{eq:normalized-modulus}
 S_{v_t}(x,b(r))\subset x+B_r
 \qquad\bigl(x\in B_{1/4},\ r>0\bigr).$$ In particular, with $\gamma=(4R)^{-1}$, $$\label{eq:uniform-strict-convexity}
 S_u(x,tb(r))\subset x+rS_u(0,t)
 \qquad
 \bigl(x\in\gamma S_u(0,t),\ r,t>0\bigr).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-normalized-modulus"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-normalized-modulus</summary>

<a id="eq:fixed-radius-gap"></a>
*Proof.* Write $v=v_t$. Since $v(0)=Dv(0)=0$ and $v\le1$ on $\overline{B_1}$, convexity gives $0\le v(x)\le1/4$ on $B_{1/4}$. Fix such an $x$. For every unoriented line through $x$, choose its unit direction $e$ so that $Dv(x)\cdot e\le0$. The ray in this direction meets $\partial S_v(0,1)$ at a distance at most $2R$. At that intersection, its tangent-subtracted gap $$g_{x,e}(r)=v(x+re)-v(x)-r\,Dv(x)\cdot e$$ is at least $1-v(x)\ge3/4$. Thus the positive-ray radius of the closed tangent section of height $1/4$, based at $x$, is at most $2R$. Balance of that section bounds the opposite-ray radius by $2R/\rho$: a point on the opposite ray, reflected and contracted by $\rho$, must still fit on the first ray. Consequently every unit direction $e$ satisfies $$\label{eq:fixed-radius-gap}
 g_{x,e}(R_*)\ge\frac14,\qquad R_*=\frac{2R}{\rho}.$$

The gap $g_{x,e}$ is nondecreasing on the positive ray. Define <a id="eq:explicit-modulus"></a>$$\label{eq:explicit-modulus}
 N(r)=\max\left\{0,\left\lceil\log_\beta\frac{R_*}{r}\right\rceil\right\},
 \qquad b(r)=\frac{1}{8A^{N(r)}}.$$ Iterating <a href="#eq:gap-doubling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gap-doubling">[eq:gap-doubling]</a> and using <a href="#eq:fixed-radius-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-radius-gap">[eq:fixed-radius-gap]</a> gives $$A^{N(r)}g_{x,e}(r)
 \ge g_{x,e}\bigl(\beta^{N(r)}r\bigr)
 \ge\frac14.$$ Thus the tangent gap is at least $2b(r)$ at every point of distance $r$ from $x$, and at every point farther away. This proves <a href="#eq:normalized-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-modulus">[eq:normalized-modulus]</a>.

If $x\in\gamma S_v(0,1)$, then $|x|<1/4$. Since $B_1\subset S_v(0,1)$, we obtain $$S_v(x,b(r))\subset x+B_r\subset x+rS_v(0,1).$$ Applying $A_t$ gives <a href="#eq:uniform-strict-convexity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-strict-convexity">[eq:uniform-strict-convexity]</a>. All constants and the function $b$ are independent of the height $t$, the base $x$ in the stated range, and the line direction. ◻

</details>

Condition <a href="#eq:uniform-strict-convexity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-strict-convexity">[eq:uniform-strict-convexity]</a> is precisely the uniform strict convexity condition of (Trudinger and Wang 2000, Condition (4.10)) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[TW2000](#ref-TW2000)], with base point zero. For the final step we use its underlying interior estimate, so that the effect of the section rescaling is explicit.

<a id="stmt-thm-tw-interior"></a>
<div id="thm:tw-interior" class="theorem">

**Theorem 20** (Trudinger–Wang interior estimate). *Let $O\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded convex domain and let $f\in C^4(O)$ have positive-definite Hessian and solve the affine maximal equation. Suppose $-1\le f\le0$. If a positive function $b_0$ gives the section control $$S_{f|_O}(x,b_0(r))\subset B_r(x)
 \qquad(x\in O,\ r>0),$$ then $f\in C^\infty(O)$, and for each $O'\Subset O$ and integer $q\ge2$ there are positive constants $c,C_q$ such that $$D^2f\ge c\mathrm{Id},\qquad |D^qf|\le C_q
 \quad\hbox{on }O'.$$ The constants depend only on $n$, $\operatorname{diam}O$, $\operatorname{dist}(O',\partial O)$, and the specified lower modulus $b_0$, with additional dependence on $q$ for $C_q$.*

</div>

This is (Trudinger and Wang 2000, Theorem 4.2) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[TW2000](#ref-TW2000)], stated in terms of a prescribed lower bound for the modulus of convexity. Its dimension is arbitrary. The sections $S_{f|_O}$ here are taken within $O$. Its proof combines the linearized Monge–Ampère estimates of Caffarelli and Gutiérrez (Caffarelli and Gutiérrez 1997) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[CaffarelliGutierrez1997](#ref-CaffarelliGutierrez1997)], Caffarelli’s Monge–Ampère regularity theory (Caffarelli 1990) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Caffarelli1990](#ref-Caffarelli1990)], and Schauder estimates; see (Trudinger and Wang 2000, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[TW2000](#ref-TW2000)]. The rescaling in the following proof is the reduction in (Trudinger and Wang 2000, Theorem 2.1 and its proof) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[TW2000](#ref-TW2000)], with the uniform modulus now supplied by Lemma <a href="#lem:normalized-modulus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:normalized-modulus">19</a>.

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-tw-interior"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-tw-interior</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Proposition <a href="#prop:one-direction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:one-direction">14</a> applies for every $3\le n\le9$. Lemmas <a href="#lem:uniform-balance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:uniform-balance">17</a> and <a href="#lem:doubling-entire" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:doubling-entire">18</a> therefore give $\Omega=\mathbb R^n$ and uniform section balance. Fix any base point, translate it to zero, subtract its tangent, and use the normalized functions $v_t$ from <a href="#eq:normalized-sections" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-sections">[eq:normalized-sections]</a>.

These functions solve the same affine maximal equation. Indeed, for $v(y)=t^{-1}u(Ay)$, set $$c_t=t^{-n}(\det A)^2,\qquad a=\frac{n+1}{n+2}.$$ Then $$w_v=c_t^{-a}w_u\circ A,\qquad
 U_v=t^{1-n}(\det A)^2 A^{-1}(U_u\circ A)A^{-\mathsf T},$$ so the contraction $U_v^{ij}(w_v)_{ij}$ is a nonzero constant multiple of $(U_u^{ij}(w_u)_{ij})\circ A$, and vanishes. Subtracting an affine function leaves the equation unchanged as well.

<a id="eq:normalized-derivative-bounds"></a>
Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:tw-interior" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tw-interior">20</a> to $f_t=v_t-1$ on $O=B_{1/4}$, with $O'=B_{1/8}$. There $-1\le f_t\le-3/4$, and Lemma <a href="#lem:normalized-modulus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:normalized-modulus">19</a> supplies the same lower modulus at every point of $O$. Restricting a section to $O$ only decreases it. We obtain constants independent of $t$ such that $$\label{eq:normalized-derivative-bounds}
 D^2v_t(0)\ge c_0\mathrm{Id},\qquad |D^3v_t(0)|\le C_0.$$

<a id="eq:inverse-normalization-bound"></a>
Put $H=D^2u(0)>0$. The identity $$D^2v_t(0)=t^{-1}A_t^{\mathsf T}HA_t$$ and the first bound in <a href="#eq:normalized-derivative-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-derivative-bounds">[eq:normalized-derivative-bounds]</a> imply $$t c_0|z|^2
 \le (A_tz)^{\mathsf T}H(A_tz)
 \le\lambda_{\max}(H)|A_tz|^2,$$ and hence $$\label{eq:inverse-normalization-bound}
 \|A_t^{-1}\|
 \le\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(H)/c_0}\,t^{-1/2}.$$ For any three vectors $z_1,z_2,z_3$, differentiation of $u(x)=t\,v_t(A_t^{-1}x)$ gives $$D^3u(0)[z_1,z_2,z_3]
 =t\,D^3v_t(0)
       [A_t^{-1}z_1,A_t^{-1}z_2,A_t^{-1}z_3].$$ Equations <a href="#eq:normalized-derivative-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-derivative-bounds">[eq:normalized-derivative-bounds]</a> and <a href="#eq:inverse-normalization-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inverse-normalization-bound">[eq:inverse-normalization-bound]</a> therefore give $$|D^3u(0)|\le Ct^{-1/2}\longrightarrow0
 \qquad\text{as }t\longrightarrow\infty.$$ The base point was arbitrary, so $D^3u\equiv0$. Thus $$u(x)=\tfrac12 x^{\mathsf T}Qx+b\cdot x+c$$ on $\mathbb R^n$, with $Q>0$. Its graph is an invertible affine image of $\{(z,|z|^2):z\in\mathbb R^n\}$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The affine-complete consequence

The graph theorem now gives the consequence announced in the introduction. The completeness and global graph results of Trudinger and Wang represent the hypersurface as a complete convex graph.

<a id="stmt-cor-affine-complete"></a>
<div id="cor:affine-complete" class="corollary">

**Corollary 21** (Affine-complete hypersurfaces). *Let $3\le n\le9$. Let $M$ be a smooth locally uniformly convex immersed hypersurface in $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ whose underlying manifold is connected and open (noncompact and without boundary). Suppose that $M$ is complete for the affine (Berwald–Blaschke) metric and is classically affine maximal (stationary for affine area under compactly supported smooth variations). Then $M$ is an invertible affine image of the standard elliptic paraboloid.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-affine-complete"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-affine-complete</summary>

*Proof.* By (Trudinger and Wang 2002, Theorem A) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[TW2002](#ref-TW2002)], $M$ is complete for the induced Euclidean metric. By (Trudinger and Wang 2002, Corollary 1) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[TW2002](#ref-TW2002)], an invertible affine change of coordinates gives an affine image $\widetilde M$ that is the global graph of a convex function $u$ over a nonempty open convex domain $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$, not assumed to equal $\mathbb R^n$. The linear part $L$ of this change uniformly compares Euclidean lengths by its least and greatest singular values, preserving completeness. By Equation <a href="#eq:area-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:area-covariance">[eq:area-covariance]</a>, it multiplies affine area on compact patches by the fixed positive factor $|\det L|^{n/(n+2)}$, preserving stationarity. Smoothness and local uniform convexity give $u\in C^\infty(\Omega)$ with positive-definite Hessian, and classical stationarity gives <a href="#eq:affine-maximal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-maximal">[eq:affine-maximal]</a>. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> now implies $\Omega=\mathbb R^n$ and that $u$ is a quadratic polynomial with positive-definite Hessian. Undoing the affine change of coordinates proves the conclusion. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The change from $(y_{1},y′)$ to $(r,z)$ at the fixed base point $s = 1$, shown schematically with one $y′$ coordinate. The actual fiber need not have the smooth or symmetric shape drawn here. The slice $y_{1} = ϵ$ becomes the slice $r = 1$, while $A_{ϵ}$ normalizes its remaining transverse coordinates. The interior point $(1,0)$ of $F$ is sent to $(ϵ^{−1},0)$. The right panel shows only this point, the origin, and the distinguished slice; the shape of the transformed fiber is omitted.",
      "figure_id": "fig:thin-slice",
      "pdf_page": 7,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:thin-slice",
          "exact_quote": "The change from $(y_1,y')$ to $(r,z)$ at the fixed base\npoint $s=\\mathbf1$, shown schematically with one $y'$ coordinate.\nThe actual fiber need not have the smooth or symmetric shape drawn here.\nThe slice $y_1=\\epsilon$ becomes the slice $r",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,>=stealth,\n  every node/.style={font=\\small},line cap=round]\n  \\begin{scope}[shift={(0,0)}]\n    \\fill[blue!5] (0,1.25) ellipse[x radius=1.18,y radius=1.25];\n    \\draw[thick] (0,1.25) ellipse[x radius=1.18,y radius=1.25];\n    \\draw[->,gray] (-1.65,0)--(1.75,0) node[right,black] {$y'$};\n    \\draw[->,gray] (0,-.24)--(0,2.85) node[above,black] {$y_1$};\n    \\draw[densely dashed,gray] (-1.46,.44)--(1.43,.44);\n    \\draw[very thick,blue!60!black] (-.899,.44)--(.899,.44);\n    \\node[left] at (-1.46,.44) {$\\epsilon$};\n    \\node[anchor=south west,blue!60!black] at (.27,.49) {$F_\\epsilon$};\n    \\fill (0,0) circle[radius=1.3pt];\n    \\node[below left] at (0,0) {$0$};\n    \\fill (0,1.25) circle[radius=1.3pt];\n    \\node[anchor=west] at (.08,1.25) {$(1,0)$};\n    \\node at (-.52,1.8) {$F$};\n    \\node[align=center] at (0,-.7) {Original fiber\\\\$s=\\mathbf1$};\n  \\end{scope}\n  \\draw[->,thick] (2.15,1.32)--(4.15,1.32);\n  \\node[align=center] at (3.15,1.84)\n    {$r=y_1/\\epsilon$\\\\$z=A_\\epsilon y'$};\n  \\begin{scope}[shift={(6,0)}]\n    \\draw[->,gray] (-1.6,0)--(1.8,0) node[right,black] {$z$};\n    \\draw[->,gray] (0,-.24)--(0,2.85) node[above,black] {$r$};\n    \\draw[densely dashed,gray] (-1.43,.92)--(1.43,.92);\n    \\draw[very thick,blue!60!black] (-1.16,.92)--(1.16,.92);\n    \\node[left] at (-1.43,.92) {$1$};\n    \\node[anchor=south west,blue!60!black] at (.14,.99)\n      {$A_\\epsilon F_\\epsilon$};\n    \\fill (0,0) circle[radius=1.3pt];\n    \\node[below left] at (0,0) {$0$};\n    \\fill (0,2.32) circle[radius=1.3pt];\n    \\node[anchor=west] at (.08,2.32) {$(\\epsilon^{-1},0)$};\n    \\node[align=center] at (0,-.7) {Rescaled slice\\\\$s=\\mathbf1$};\n  \\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two-panel schematic diagram showing coordinate rescaling:\n- Left panel (\"Original fiber\", \"$s = 1$\"): Cartesian axes labeled $y_1$ (vertical, pointing up) and $y'$ (horizontal, pointing right) with origin labeled $0$. An oval/egg-shaped domain $F$ contains the origin and an interior point on the vertical axis marked with a dot and labeled $(1, 0)$. A horizontal dashed line at height $\\epsilon$ on the $y_1$-axis marks the slice labeled $F_\\epsilon$.\n- Center: Rescaling equations $r = y_1/\\epsilon$ and $z = A_\\epsilon y'$.\n- Right panel (\"Rescaled slice\", \"$s = 1$\"): Cartesian axes labeled $r$ (vertical, pointing up) and $z$ (horizontal, pointing right) with origin labeled $0$. A tick at $1$ on the $r$-axis contains a horizontal line segment representing the slice labeled $A_\\epsilon F_\\epsilon$. Higher on the $r$-axis is a point marked with a dot and labeled $(\\epsilon^{-1}, 0)$."
    }
  ],
